In the center, a white pelican is depicted "in its piety" <note>A traditional heraldic and alchemical symbol where a mother pelican wounds her own breast to feed her young with her blood, representing self-sacrifice and the nurturing of spiritual life</note>, pecking its breast to feed its young, while standing in a golden crescent. ->HERMETIC PHILOSOPHY<-
<note>original: "PHILOSOPHIA • HERMETICA"</note>

<term>Hermetic Philosophy</term> (Hermeticism): A philosophical and esoteric tradition based on writings attributed to Hermes Trismegistus. It explores the hidden connections between the human soul (the microcosm) and the universe (the macrocosm).

<term>Pelican in its piety</term>: In the context of alchemy, this symbol represents the "Great Work" of transformation and the stage of "multiplication," where the spiritual essence is refined and shared.

<term>Rosicrucian imagery</term>: Symbols such as the rose and the cross used by the Rosicrucians, a secret society of mystics and philosophers who sought to reform the world through divine wisdom and science.

<summary>This frontispiece features a symbolic emblem of a pelican feeding its young atop a pedestal of roses, identifying the manuscript as a work of Hermetic philosophy. These symbols are central to Rosicrucian and alchemical traditions, representing sacrifice, rebirth, and hidden knowledge.</summary>

->Macarius the Egyptian.<- <note>Macarius the Egyptian (c. 300–391 AD) was a monk and hermit. These spiritual homilies, though traditionally attributed to him, are now often associated with a "Pseudo-Macarius," likely from the Syrian tradition, and were highly valued by mystics for their focus on the internal "heart."</note>

->Cornelis Kiel.<- <note>Cornelis Kiel (c. 1528–1607), often known as Kiliaan, was a celebrated lexicographer and proofreader for the Plantin Press; he authored the first truly scholarly Dutch dictionary.</note>

<image-desc>The woodcut printer's device of Christopher Plantin. It depicts a hand emerging from a cloud and holding a pair of compasses, which is drawing a circle on a ground line. This is enclosed within an oval border containing the motto "By Labor and Constancy" <note>Original Latin: "LABORE ET CONSTANTIA." This famous motto reflects Plantin's work ethic: the moving leg of the compass represents labor, while the fixed leg represents constancy.</note>. The oval is surrounded by a decorative frame featuring floral arrangements in vases and scrollwork.</image-desc>

->AT ANTWERP,<-
->By Christopher Plantin/ Printer to the<-
->Royal Majesty. 1580.<- <note>Plantin was appointed the "Prototypographus Regius" (King's Printer) by Philip II of Spain, a prestigious and powerful position in the publishing world of the Spanish Netherlands.</note>

# To the Well-Disposed
## Reader.

<image-desc>An ornamental historiated initial 'H' featuring a seated figure amidst scrolling foliage.</image-desc>

ALTHOUGH, well-disposed Reader, the <term>blindness<gloss>verblindtheydt</gloss></term> arising from the <term>arrogance<gloss>opgheblasenheydt</gloss></term> and <term>presumption<gloss>vermetenheydt</gloss></term> of men has brought forth a great number of <term>sects and heresies<gloss>secten ende ketterijen</gloss></term> within <term>Christendom<gloss>Christenheydt</gloss></term>: nevertheless, among all these, there is no arch-heretic who would dare to affirm in words that faith is not necessary for the <term>salvation<gloss>salicheydt</gloss></term> of man. <note>The author argues that while everyone agrees faith is necessary, very few actually agree on what that faith should be.</note> But how small is the number of those who understand, or who truly wish to humble themselves, in order to understand and know what one must believe; truly, the fruits and works that daily come from this prove as much. For the disputes, discords, divisions, hatreds, and feuds through which we clearly see that every man emboldens himself to separate from the unity of the <term>Catholic Church<gloss>Catholijcker Kercken</gloss></term> and from brotherly love: indeed, also to condemn, to damn, to deceive, to drive away, and to utterly destroy all those who do not agree with what seems good to him according to his own mind. All these things take their origin from the veins of an inward <term>mistrust<gloss>waentrouwicheyt</gloss></term> and unbelief toward God, springing from a haughty presumption of oneself, through which man allows himself to imagine—

<summary>In this preface, the author laments the rise of religious sects and discord within Christendom, attributing these divisions to human pride and a lack of true humility before the unity of the Catholic Church.</summary>

<keywords>Catholic Church, heresy, Reformation, religious conflict, humility, salvation, Christendom, pride</keywords>

Indeed, in order to hold fast to his <term>fantasies and imaginings<gloss>fantasijen ende inbeeldingen</gloss></term>, which he allows himself to think is the necessary faith to reach <term>salvation<gloss>salicheydt</gloss></term>: he emboldens himself to bring forward, for the confirmation of his intended <term>righteousness<gloss>gherechticheyt</gloss></term>, the testimonies of the certain faith brought forth by the <term>holy Fathers<gloss>heylighe Vaders</gloss></term> <note>The "holy Fathers" refers to early Christian theologians and bishops, such as Augustine or Jerome, whose writings were used as authoritative benchmarks for doctrine.</note>. ## To the Well-Disposed Reader.

...so to speak, against all human reason and intellect, indeed even against all the appearance and signs of the adherence to the <term>oral ordinances<gloss>mondelicke ordinacien</gloss></term> <note>The "oral ordinances" refer to the Jewish tradition of the Oral Law, which Paul, as a Pharisee, would have followed with great rigor.</note> of the law. As this is clearly and openly evident in <term>Saint Paul<gloss>Sinte Pauwels</gloss></term> himself, who—notwithstanding all his diligence, observances, and zeal for the law of his forefathers, and being therefore flaming and fiery in threats and bloodshed—having desired letters from the High Priest to conduct an <term>inquisition<gloss>inquisitie</gloss></term> <note>Here, "inquisition" refers to a formal judicial search or inquiry into the activities of the early Christians.</note>, investigation, and persecution against the disciples of Christ (which was nonetheless the ultimate intent of the law), surely thought he was doing very well to go to <term>Damascus<gloss>Damasck</gloss></term> to carry out the execution. 

He remained in that opinion and will until, as he approached those places, he was struck to the earth by the light of the light shining from heaven and fell from all his sensory perceptions and intentions. And having been thus humbled, brought low, trembling, terrified, and finding himself blind, he placed himself entirely in the will of the Lord. Following this, without returning to his former diligences, zeals for the law, and human cleverness according to the letter, he allowed himself to be willingly led and guided to the knowledge of the <term>Divinity<gloss>Godtheydt</gloss></term> of the Lord. Proceeding faithfully from this point, he was filled with the <term>Holy Spirit<gloss>Heylighen Gheest</gloss></term> and made a faithful and up-

## To the Well-Disposed Reader.

<term>true servant<gloss>recht dienaer</gloss></term> of our Lord **Jesus Christ**, and ordained by the same as a preacher, apostle, and teacher of the <term>Gentiles<gloss>Heydenen</gloss></term>, suffering for the sake of his service all kinds of contempt and torments, without being in any way ashamed of it: wherefore he could rightly say afterward, but not before: *I know in whom I have believed, and am certain of his power, etc.* <note>original: "Ick weet in wien dat ick heb ghelooft, ende ben seker van sijne macht, &c." This is a reference to 2 Timothy 1:12.</note>

Wherein it is clearly to be noticed that for a person to be <term>blessed<gloss>salich</gloss></term> and happy in **God**, it is not enough that he believes in Him according to the <term>literal history<gloss>letterlicke historie</gloss></term> <note>The author argues that knowing the "literal history"—the stories of the Bible as factual events—is insufficient for salvation without a deeper, personal spiritual transformation.</note> and in His works which are wonderfully presented and accomplished in those who have been obedient to His commandments. Rather, we must of necessity, upon hearing His voice, entirely deny ourselves, and each one completely depart from himself, renouncing our own lusts, desires, wisdoms, ingenuities, diligences, cleverness, and <term>fleshly exercises<gloss>vleeschelicke oeffeninghen</gloss></term> <note>These "fleshly exercises" refer to human-driven efforts or external rituals performed without true spiritual inspiration.</note>: in order to humbly, purely, simply, and securely place our trust in the **grace** and firm promises of the same **God**. 

Him we then begin to know as <term>almighty<gloss>almachtich</gloss></term> and our heavenly Father; and following His natural goodness, like good children, we begin to wish, to desire, and to pray with a firm confidence that His holy name, His power, and His working might be made holy in us, so that His heavenly

Using Saint Paul as an example, the text encourages readers to move beyond outward "fleshly" efforts to a humble, internal trust in God as a heavenly Father.</summary>

### ->To the Well-Disposed Reader.<-

Christ <note>original: "Christi"</note>, have obtained this true faith, so that they might therein demonstrate and practice virtue; in virtue, discretion <note>The original Dutch uses "bescheedenheydt," which in this 16th-century context of 2 Peter 1 refers to spiritual knowledge or discernment</note>; in discretion, temperance; in temperance, patience; in patience, godliness; in godliness, brotherly love; and in brotherly love, universal love. For if these things (he says) are in you abundantly, they shall not leave you idle or unfruitful in the knowledge of our Lord Jesus Christ. Therefore, brothers, be all the more diligent, so that through good works you may make your calling and election sure. For by doing this, you shall never sin: seeing that in this way an entrance shall be abundantly prepared for you into the eternal kingdom of our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ. <margin>1 John 1</margin> Saint John <note>original: "Sint Ian"</note>, in the first chapter of his first letter, speaks thus: He who says that he has fellowship with God, and walks in darkness, is untruthful. <margin>1 John 2</margin> And in the second chapter he says: By this we know that we have known him, if we keep his commandments. He who loves his brother remains in the light, and in him there is no cause for stumbling <note>original: "argherniffe," referring to a spiritual offense or a trap that causes one to fall into sin</note>, etc. Shortly after he says further: The world passes away with its lusts; but whoever does the will of God remains for eternity. Children, the last hour is here, and just as you have heard that the Antichrist <note>original: "Antechrift"</note> is coming, even now many antichrists have appeared; and by this we know that it is the last time. ### To the Kind-Hearted Reader.

Seeing that these and similar writings of the holy Fathers <term>holy Fathers: "heyligher Vaderen," referring to early Christian theologians and monks, likely including Macarius the Egyptian</term> are very useful and necessary in these perilous times: because nowadays the abundance of wickedness grows more and more and becomes greater: which is the reason (as the Apostle himself also testifies) that the love in many people grows cold and fades <note>A reference to Matthew 24:12, though the author attributes the sentiment to "the Apostle," a title usually reserved for St. Paul</note>. Therefore, kind-hearted reader, please accept this with gratitude, for it has been done out of a good heart and brotherly love, in the hope that many common, simple folk <term>common, simple folk: "ghemeyne slechte lieden"; in this period, "slechte" meant plain, humble, or ordinary rather than "bad"</term>, who through the stirring of the spirit seek help and support inwardly, may be refreshed, comforted, and strengthened through these spiritual Homilies <term>Homilies: "Homilien," religious discourses or sermons focused on spiritual growth</term>. Let us unanimously pray to the Almighty God, that it may please him to enlighten us all together and to endow us with his grace, through his eternal and only son our Lord and Savior <term>Savior: "Salichmaecker," literally "one who makes [the soul] blessed or whole"</term> Jesus Christ <note>original: "Iefum Christum"</note>, in the unity of his Holy Spirit <note>original: "Heylighen Gheests"</note>, for eternity <note>original: "eevvicheydt"</note>.

What the Prophet saw in that ecstasy <note>original: "opgetoghentheyt," a state of being "carried away" or in a trance-like spiritual vision</note> of his spirit was true and certain: but it signified and showed something else hidden and divine: namely the mystery <term>mystery: here referring to a divine truth once hidden but now revealed through faith</term> which in truth has been hidden from the ages, but revealed in the last time in the coming of Christ. For the soul which is worthy to be a partaker of the spirit in the enlightenment of Christ, and which is enlightened by the adornment of His unspeakable glory—when Christ shall have made her ready to be His dwelling-place—she becomes entirely light, entirely a face, and entirely an eye: nor is there any part in her that is not full of spiritual, enlightened eyes. That is to say, she has nothing dark in her, but becomes altogether light and spirit, all full of eyes: nor does she have any "back" part on the rear, but shows a face from all sides, because the unspeakable beauty of the glory of the light of Christ enters into her and remains there. 

And just as the sun is uniform throughout, without having a back part or a bottom part, but shines and glimmers entirely and wholly with light, being wholly light and made of equal parts: Or just as in a fire, the light of the fire is entirely like itself, and has nothing within itself that is first or last, or greater or lesser: So also the soul that is perfectly enlightened and adorned by the unspeakable beauty of the glory of the light of the face of Christ, and which is filled with the Holy Spirit, and is worthy to become the dwelling-place and the throne of God, becomes entirely eye, light, face, glory and spirit: namely, when Christ so adorns, carries, drives, conducts, and leads her, and in this manner instructs her and beautifies her with spiritual beauty. The four animals that pull the chariot <note>original: "wagē," referring to the Merkabah or divine chariot described in the Book of Ezekiel</note> represent the fundamental and rational parts of the soul. For just as the Eagle is the highest among the birds, and the Lion among the wild animals, the Bull among the tame animals, and Man among the creatures: so there are certain parts of the soul that are most prominent through the understanding; namely: will, conscience, spirit, and the power of love. For through these the chariot of the soul is governed, and God rests therein. According to another interpretation <note>original: "verstāt," literally "understanding" or "sense"</note>, this can also signify the heavenly gathering of the saints. For just as it is said that those animals were raised very high and were full of eyes, and that no one could grasp the number or the height of those eyes, because it was not permitted to know them—and just as all people are allowed to behold and wonder at the stars, but no human may know their total number—so too it is granted to all who desire to struggle <note>original: "strijden," referring to the "spiritual warfare" or the disciplined effort of a person seeking a holy life</note> to come and rest in the heavenly gathering of the saints; but to know or grasp the number of the saints belongs to God alone. Therefore, this Rider is carried in the chariot and upon the throne of the animals full of eyes, or by every soul that has become His throne and seat, and which is all eye and light when He sits within it; and He restrains it with the bridles of the Spirit, just as one who moves it knows how. And just as these spiritual animals did not go wherever they pleased, but toward wherever He who sat upon them knew and willed, and wherever He directed them: so too in this life He restrains souls, moving them with His Spirit, so that they do not go toward heaven according to their own will as they please. Rather, through His wisdom, He drives the soul into heaven when it leaves the body; and again, when He wills, it descends into the body and into the thoughts. For the Lord said to them: <margin>Matthew 5:b</margin> "You are the salt of the earth," calling the souls of men "earth," in which they have placed the heavenly salt of the Spirit, to preserve them from all corruption and to deliver them from a great stench. For just as meat, if it is not sprinkled with salt, immediately spoils and becomes foul-smelling, so that all people turn away from the very bad odor; furthermore, worms come into the decayed meat, take nourishment from it, eat it, and nest therein. But if salt is added, then the worms are driven away and those that were previously fed there perish; also, the foul stench vanishes. For such is the nature of salt, that it kills the worms and drives away the bad odor. 

Likewise, every soul that is not <term>preserved <note>original: "geconfijt," meaning preserved or seasoned, often used in the context of pickling or candying to prevent spoilage</note></term> with the Holy Spirit, or not salted with the heavenly salt—which is the power of God—decays and is full of the many stenches of evil thoughts. Thus, God's face is turned away from that heavy odor of vain and dark thoughts and desires that dwell in such a soul. And the evil and harmful worms—which are spirits of wickedness <margin><unclear>Ephesians 6</unclear></margin> and powers of darkness—walk, graze, nest, and crawl therein, eating it up and destroying it. 

For the Scripture says: <margin>Psalm 37:a <note>The author uses the Vulgate numbering; in most English Bibles, this is Psalm 38:5</note></margin> "My wounds are rotten, stinking, and have become foul." It kills those gruesome worms, drives away the bad odor, and purifies the soul through the power of His might. And thus, being healed through the benefit of that true salt and delivered from corruption, it is ordained for the use and service of the heavenly Lord. # Of the Sincerity of Christians.

<margin>Sacrifice.</margin>
...sprinkled with salt. The sacrifice had to first be killed by the Priests, and afterwards be divided and sprinkled with salt, and finally laid upon the fire. For if the Priest had not first killed the animal, it would not have been salted, nor would it have been offered to God as a <note>original: "rueck der soetichept," a Dutch rendering of the Latin biblical phrase "odor suavitatis," often translated as "sweet-smelling savor."</note> sweet-smelling savor. In the same way, our soul, when it comes to Christ the true High Priest, must be killed by Him; it must die to evil desires and to the former wicked life—that is, to sins—and the malice of evil desires must perish from within it. For just as a body is dead when the soul is separated from it, and no longer lives the life it once lived—it does not hear, nor does it walk—so also when Christ, the heavenly Priest, has killed our worldly life through the grace of His power, it is brought to nothing regarding the evil life it led. It no longer hears, nor speaks, nor does it dwell any longer in the darkness of sin, because the malice of the <term>affections <note>original: "affectien," referring to disordered passions or emotional inclinations that drive a person toward sin.</note></term>, which was like its very soul, has passed away through grace.

<margin>Galatians 6:14</margin>
Which the Apostle calls out, saying: "The world is crucified to me, and I to the world." For the soul that remains firmly alive in the world and in the darkness of sin has not yet been put to death by Him, but still has within itself the "soul of wickedness"—that is, the power of darkness, which is produced by the desires of sin and is fed by them. It does not belong to the body of Christ, nor to the body of light, but it is the body of darkness and remains in darkness. Just as those who are endowed with the "soul of light"—that is, with the power of the Holy Spirit—remain in that light. # Homily I.

...understand. Just as the garment which you wear was made by another, but you have put it on; and just as another has built and constructed the house, but you dwell therein: so also when Adam transgressed the commandment of God, and became obedient to the evil serpent, and sold himself to the devil, the Devil then "put on" his soul—that is, that very pure <term>creature <note>In this context, "creature" (Dutch: *creatuere*) refers to anything created by God, specifically the human soul as a created being.</note></term> which God had created after His own image. Just as also the
<margin>Colossians 2:15</margin>
Apostle says: Having spoiled the principalities and powers, He has triumphed over them on the Cross. For this reason the Lord has come: that He might cast them out and might receive His own house and His own temple, namely, man. Therefore the soul is said to be the "body of darkness" and of malice, as long as the darkness of sin is within it; for then it lives in the dark wickedness which has 
<margin>
Romans 6:6
Romans 7:24
</margin>
possessed it. Just as Paul, calling it the "body of sin" and the "body of death," says: "That the body of sin might be destroyed." And again: "Who shall deliver me from the body of this death?" On the contrary, the soul that has believed in God and is delivered from sin—having killed the life of darkness and having received the light of the Holy Spirit as its life—remains persevering thereafter, for it is governed by the divine light. For the soul is neither of the divine nature, nor also of the nature of darkness and of malice; but it is an <term>intellectual creature <note>original: "verstantbaere creature," referring to a being endowed with reason and understanding.</note></term>, beautiful, chosen, wonderful, and very pure—the likeness and the image of God. Through the original transgression, the malice of dark desires entered into it. Furthermore, under whichever dominion the soul exists, it is clinging and united to that one through its own will. If it then has the divine light within it and lives therein, it is abundant in all virtues of light and of rest; but if it is burdened with the darkness of sin, it shall receive damnation.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul's transformation, moving from the internal "garment" of sin described on the previous page to the necessity of a total spiritual relocation. ### On the uprightness of Christians.

Thus, the soul that has set its purpose to live with God, to enter into eternal rest and into eternal light—as was said before—ought to go to Christ, the true High Priest, and be put to death to the world and to the previous life of darkness and malice. It must be transferred into another life and into another divine <term>upbringing <note>The Dutch *opvoedinghe* implies both education and the way one is raised or nurtured in a new environment.</note></term>. Just as one who has died in a city no longer hears the voices or the words of those who live there, nor the noise of what happens there, but is completely dead and transferred to another place where no such voices or calls exist as were in the city: so also the soul, when it is put to death and has died to this community of the "city" of evil <term>affections <note>In this context, 'affections' (*affectiën*) refers to the deep-seated desires and emotional inclinations that drive human behavior.</note></term> where it lived before, no longer hears the voice of dark disputations near it, nor the words or the cries of idle quarreling, nor the noise of the spirits of darkness. Instead, it is transferred into a city full of <term>uprightness <note>original: "oprechticheyt." This refers to spiritual integrity, sincerity, and being "right" with God.</note></term> and peace—into the city (I say) of divine light. There it lives and hears; there it dwells, speaks, and reasons; and there it performs spiritual and God-worthy things.

Let us then also pray heartily that we may be put to death through His power, and die to the world from all the malice of darkness, and that the spirit of sin may be taken from us. Let us pray that we may put on and receive into the soul a heavenly spirit, and that we may be transferred from the malice of darkness to the light of Christ, and that we may rest in that life for all eternity. 

But just as when men run in a <term>race <note>The author uses the image of the *loopbaene* (racetrack), a common metaphor in early Christian writing for the disciplined pursuit of spiritual goals.</note></term>, the chariot in front hinders, prevents, and delays the one following so that it might not come ahead and reach the prize: so also do the thoughts of the soul and of sin run within a person. If it then happens that the <unclear>thought</unclear> of sin goes before, it hinders, prevents, and delays the soul itself, so that it cannot come to God and gain victory over sin:

<meta>catchword: But</meta>

It concludes with a racing metaphor, illustrating how sinful thoughts can obstruct the soul’s progress toward God.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 8 regarding the soul's transformation. # Homily 1.

But if the Lord Himself sits upon it and moves the soul, He always triumphs, leading and wisely driving the <term>chariot of the soul <note>The author uses the image of a "wagon" (wagen) to represent the human soul, drawing on the biblical imagery of Ezekiel’s chariot vision.</note></term> everywhere toward heavenly and divine wisdom. For He does not merely struggle against malice, but being the Master through His own power and strength, He always achieves victory. 

Thus the <term>Cherubim <note>Referring to the winged heavenly beings in Ezekiel 1 and 10, often interpreted in mystical tradition as the faculties of the soul moved by God.</note></term> are driven not where they themselves wish to go, but wherever the Sitter or <term>Charioteer <note>original: "voer-man." This refers to Christ acting as the pilot or driver of the soul's path.</note></term> directs them; and where it pleases Him, there they go, and He sustains them. For (he says) the hand of a man was beneath them. Holy souls are moved by the Spirit of Christ, who leads them where He wills and as it pleases Him—at times through heavenly thoughts, at others through the body; wherever He desires, there they serve Him. Just as the feet of birds are aided by wings, so too the heavenly light of the Spirit takes up the wings of thoughts worthy of the soul, leading and guiding them as the Spirit knows best. 

Therefore, when you hear this, consider for yourself whether you possess this in practice and in truth within your soul. For these are not merely words plainly spoken, but rather the work of truth brought forth within the soul. If, then, you have not obtained this, but find yourself in need of these great spiritual blessings, you must be constantly anxious and sorrowful, and you must labor as one who is still dead to the Kingdom. Like one wounded, you must always call out to the Lord and desire with confidence that you may be made worthy of this <term>true life <note>original: "warrachtigen leuen." This signifies a spiritual existence fueled by grace rather than mere biological life.</note></term>. 

For just as when God created the body, He did not grant it the power to derive life, food, drink, clothing, or footwear from its own nature or from within the body itself; but rather, having created it as a naked body in itself, He provided it with everything necessary for life from the outside. Thus, the body could not live without that which is external to it—that is, without food, drink, and—

# Homily.

[Ange]ls feel sorrow for those souls who do not partake of the 
heavenly and spiritual food, and who do not live
in immortality. These (I assure you once more) are
not empty words, but the working of the spiritual
life, the work of truth, which is fulfilled 
in the worthy and faithful soul. For this
reason, you have become the throne of God, and
the <term>heavenly charioteer <note>original: "voer-man." A common image in early Christian mysticism, particularly in the Macarian homilies, where God or the Holy Spirit is the driver who guides the "chariot" of the soul.</note></term> sits upon you; and your soul 
has entirely become a spiritual eye, and
has been wholly transformed into light: if it is also true that you 
have tasted that spiritual food, and have drunk from
that living water, and that you have put on the 
clothing of the hidden light; indeed, if your 
<term>inner, disciplined man <note>original: "inwendighe gheoeffende mensch." This refers to the "inner self" (Ephesians 3:16) that has been trained through spiritual practice and devotion.</note></term> is overflowing with these things: 
behold, then you undoubtedly live an eternal life now, 
since your soul henceforth rests with the Lord. Behold, 
you have truly obtained and received this from the 
Lord, so that you may live the true 
life. If you do not feel any of these things 
within you, then weep, be distressed, and lament; for
without a doubt, you have not yet obtained the 
spiritual and eternal riches, nor have you 
yet received the true life. Therefore, be 
mindful of your frailty, praying to God 
night and day; for you are placed in the heavy 
poverty of sin. Oh, if only there were someone who was 
distressed and anxious because of their lack, and that we did 
not live so complacently like satisfied people! For he 
who suffers such distress and prays to God 
without ceasing shall quickly obtain deliverance and 
heavenly riches; just as the Lord said when He 
spoke of the unjust judge and the widow: 
How much more shall God <term>avenge <note>original: "wraecke doen." In the context of the Parable of the Unjust Judge (Luke 18), this refers to God bringing about justice or vindication for His people.</note></term> <margin>Luke 18:b.</margin>
those who cry out to Him night and day! 
Truly I say to you, that He will avenge them 
speedily. To Him be glory and majesty for eternity.

# Homily II.

The Lord, clothed with the garments of the kingdom of the **hidden light** / with the garments of faith / of hope / of love / of joy / of peace / of goodness / of kindness / and with other such garments of light and of life / with godly and living garments / full of unspeakable rest: so that just as God is love / joy / peace / goodness and kindness: so the <term>new man <note>A theological term for the transformed nature of a believer, contrasting with the "old man" or sinful nature mentioned on the previous page.</note></term> may also become such through grace and mercy. Glory be to his mercy and compassion / for he pities his servants / and enlightens and delivers them from the kingdom of darkness / and gives them generously his light and his kingdom. To him be glory and dominion into eternity. AMEN.

# Of the Sincerity of Christians.

...and that those who humbly pray to God / and those who do their diligence in reading / and those who labor / all living together in purity and simplicity / may be helped by one another. <margin>Matthew 6:b</margin> For to what end is it written: **Thy will be done on earth as it is in heaven.** <note>The author uses the Lord's Prayer to argue that earthly community should mirror the perfect harmony of the celestial realm.</note> Truly, just as in heaven the angels agree with one another in all harmony / in peace and love / without any pride or envy reigning there / but are joined together in love and purity: so should the brothers also associate with one another. If it happens that thirty brothers are gathered together / it is not possible for them to remain together persistently all day and night: but some of them occupy themselves with prayer for six hours / and then wish to read: others serve with a willing heart / others perform some work. Therefore, the brothers / if they do anything / must conduct themselves with one another in love and joy: And he who works or labors / must say this of the one who pours out prayers: "I am a partaker in the treasure that my brother obtains: for it is held in common." <note>This reflects the monastic ideal where spiritual "wealth" earned by one member through prayer is credited to the whole community.</note> He also who prays humbly / must think thus within himself regarding the one reading: "The profit he gathers from reading / also comes to my benefit." And he who serves / must immediately think thus: "The service I do / is for the common profit." For just as the members of the body, which are many, are one body / and help one another mutually / and each performs its own service: <margin>Romans 12:b; 1 Corinthians 12:c</margin> namely / the eye sees for the whole body / the hand works for all the members / the foot, wherever it extends / walks and carries all the other members / and nevertheless the other <margin>Romans 14:a-b</margin> member suffers and endures with it: So also must the brothers yield to one another: He who prays / may not judge the worker / because he does not pray:

<summary>This page describes the communal life of Christians, emphasizing that different roles—prayer, study, and manual labor—all contribute to a shared spiritual "treasure." It uses the Pauline metaphor of the Body of Christ to argue for mutual respect and harmony without judgment.</summary>
<keywords>Christian sincerity, communal life, prayer, manual labor, unity, Body of Christ, monasticism, mutual support</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Homily III regarding the mutual respect and spiritual unity required among brothers living in a community. The previous page ended with the sentiment that the service of one brother benefits the one who prays.</meta>

### Homily III.

...prays: nor may the one who works condemn the one praying, saying, "He rests, and I labor"; nor may the one who serves ju<unclear>d</unclear>ge the others: but everyone, when they do anything, must do it for the glory of God. The one who reads must embrace the one praying with love and joy, considering within themselves: "Because he prays for me." And the one who prays must think the same of the worker: "That which he does, he does for the common <term>benefit</term><note>original: "orbore" — a Middle Dutch term for utility, profit, or communal advantage.</note>." In this way, great willingness, peace, and harmony shall keep them all together in the bond of peace; and they shall interact and associate with one another in purity and simplicity, and be very pleasing to God. The most special and foremost of all is the fitting perseverance in prayer. But nonetheless, one thing is necessary: namely, that the person has the treasure in the soul, and the life which is the Lord, in the heart—whether they work, pray, or read: that they possess that imperishable possession, which is the Holy Spirit.

There are some who say that the Lord requires only the outward and public fruits from people, but that God sees the inward. This is not so: but just as a person guards themselves from outward <margin>Outward fruits and works</margin> actions, so must they also struggle and fight against thoughts. For the Lord desires of you that you be vexed and angry against yourself, that you wage war against your own understanding, so that you do not consent to or unite with evil thoughts. But beyond this—namely, to root out sin and the clinging defect—that belongs to divine power alone. For man cannot by his own strength root out sin by the root. To struggle, to resist, to wound, to strike—this lies within your power: but to root out and uproot, that is God's work alone. For if you could do that, what would...

# Of the quiet and peaceful conduct of the brothers.

...avail at the Lord’s coming. Just as the eye cannot see without light, and no one can speak without a tongue, or hear without ears, or walk without feet, or labor without hands: so you cannot be saved or enter the kingdom of heaven without Jesus. If you say, "I have committed no public <term>fornications</term><note>original: "hoereryen" — refers generally to sexual immorality or illicit acts.</note> nor adultery, I am not greedy," and regarding [other things], "I am righteous": you are deceived in this, thinking that you have fulfilled it all. For there are not only three types of sins from which a person

<margin>
War against oneself.
</margin>

must guard himself, but infinitely many. For pride, <term>frivolity</term><note>original: "lichtueerdicheydt" — can mean recklessness, levity, or a lack of seriousness in spiritual matters.</note>, unfaithfulness, hate, envy, deceit, hypocrisy—from where do these come? Must you not fight against these defects in your secret thoughts? Just as if a murderer comes to your house, he will not cease to be troublesome to you, nor will he let you live in peace: but you shall begin to resist him, and you shall strike and be struck. So must the soul resist, fight back, and oppose [sin]. Furthermore, the will which fights back and sighs from labor and anxiety begins to gain the upper hand; it falls, and stands up again. Sin casts the soul down yet again with ten or twenty storms—yes, it overcomes it and fells it to the earth—but the soul, with the passing of time, eventually reaches the point where it overcomes sin. Further, if it holds out and does not grow weary, it gradually begins to become the strongest, and to discern, and finally to win the prize against sin. But in this, if it is well observed, sin throws the person down for so long until he becomes a <term>perfect man</term><note>A reference to spiritual maturity or "reaching the full measure" of Christ.</note>, in the measure of [full] age: and then he shall completely

<margin>
Ephesians 4:c.
1 Corinthians 15:d.
</margin>

overcome death. For it stands written: "At last the enemy, death, shall be destroyed." <note>original: "Ten lesten zal vernielt worden de vyandelicke doodt." A translation of 1 Corinthians 15:26.</note> And thus shall men become the strongest and overcome the devil.

<meta>This page concludes Homily III, continuing the discussion from page 18 regarding the soul's internal struggle against a multitude of sins beyond the obvious outward ones. # Homily III.

...gain victory. If someone (as we have said before) stubbornly says, "I am not guilty of <term>fornication</term><note>original: "hoererije" — refers generally to sexual immorality.</note>, adultery, or <term>greed</term><gloss>abbreviation for greed</gloss><note>original Dutch: "ghierichz" for "ghiericheyt."</note>; that is enough for me": truly, this person has thus fought against three kinds of sins, but against the other twenty that sin holds against the soul, he has not fought; indeed, he is himself overcome. He must then strive and fight against all sorts of sins.

For the <term>intellect</term><note>original: "verstandt" — refers to the reasoning mind or the "nous," the spiritual faculty of the soul.</note>, as we have often said, is an adversary and has equal power against sin, through which it can contradict and resist [sinful] thoughts. If you say that the opposing power is stronger, and that sin holds total dominion over man, you then represent God as being unjust for having condemned human nature—which has been obedient to Satan—if Satan is more powerful and brings man down by an inevitable force.

You would have certainly made him more violent and powerful than the soul, [as if to say] "but in the end, you shall be obedient to me." Just as if a young man fought against a small child, and the defeated child were condemned because he had let himself be overcome, that would be an unjust judgment. Therefore, we firmly maintain that the intellect is an enemy of equal power. Thus, such a soul that has called upon divine help with the utmost diligence from the heart shall obtain it, not without reward; and shall be considered worthy to deserve redemption. For the battle and the wrestling are set between equals. Let us highly honor the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit for eternity.

On progress in the Christian life.
<meta>catchword: lead/or</meta>

### By what means one shall progress in the Christian life.

#### HOMILY IV.

<image-desc>Decorative initial 'D' featuring classical scrollwork and floral patterns.</image-desc>

Those who desire to lead the Christian life very perfectly must first well establish that part of the soul which we call the **intellect**<note>original: "verstant" — in this context, it refers to the "nous" or the spiritual faculty of understanding and discernment.</note>, through which we comprehend and **discern**<note>original: "onderscheydē" — to distinguish between different spiritual influences.</note>: so that when we have obtained the power to judge uprightly what is good or evil, and can openly discern what has been brought into our **pure nature**<note>original: "suyuere nature" — refers to the original, untainted state of humanity before the influence of sin.</note> from outside of nature, we may **live**<note>original: "conuerseren" — from the Latin "conversari," meaning one's conduct, manner of living, or spiritual "walk."</note> uprightly and unshakeably. And using that faculty of discernment as an eye, we should make no consent nor any covenant with evil promptings; and thus, honored with the divine gift, we may become worthy of the Lord.

Let us take an example from that which is subject to our eyes. For the **body** has some resemblance to the **soul**, and the things concerning the body resemble those concerning the soul, just as the things that are seen resemble those that are hidden. For just as the body has the eye for a **guide**<note>original: "leydt-man" — a leader or conductor.</note>, and the eye itself, through its sight, leads the whole body in the right way. Imagine one who went through woody places, full of thorns and of mud, where the wild beast has its **lair**<note>original: "wederslach" — a retreat or place where an animal haunts.</note>, where **swords are thrust**<note>original: "sweerden ghesteken zijn" — likely referring to hidden traps, sharp stakes, or spiritual dangers likened to blades.</note>, and where there are high, steep places and also many waters. Nevertheless, this traveler—very brave, diligent, and undaunted, having the eye for a guide—shall go very deliberately through these difficult places, carefully holding his garment with hands and feet on all sides, so that it is not torn among the bushes and thorns, nor stained by the mud, nor cut by the swords. And the eye leads the whole body, for it is its light, so that it does not fall from the steep places, or...

It uses the metaphor of a traveler navigating a dangerous, thorn-filled wilderness to illustrate how the soul must carefully avoid the traps and stains of sin.</summary>
<keywords>intellect, discernment, pure nature, spiritual guidance, metaphor of the eye, spiritual progress, Christian life</keywords>

## Homily IV. ##

...nature dwells: I mean the **heavenly grace** which must be clung to together with our nature / so that we may enter into the **heavenly bedchamber** <note>A mystical reference to the union of the soul with Christ, following the imagery of the Bridegroom in Matthew 25.</note> with the Lord / and obtain eternal salvation. <margin>Original Sin.</margin>
received within us the **evil of the senses** / which / having been turned into our nature through long habit and use / must be driven out again by the upright guest of our nature / which is the **heavenly gift of the Holy Spirit** / and be restored again to its former purity. For unless we—through constant desire / sanctification / faith / and prayer / turning away from this world—acquire that love of the Holy Spirit from heaven / and unless our nature clings to that love / which is the Lord / and is sanctified by that love of the Spirit from the evil with which it is stained / and that we remain persistently innocent / having diligently lived in all His commandments: we cannot obtain the kingdom of heaven. But it pleases me to bring here a subtler and deeper reasoning / according to the power that is given to me. Therefore, listen diligently. For each of these is / according to its nature / a body / whether it be angel / or soul / or spirit. For though these bodies are thin / nevertheless in substance / form and appearance / according to the subtlety of their nature / they are **subtle bodies** <note>In this historical context, "subtle" (subtijl) refers to things that are material but incredibly refined or ethereal, like air or light, as opposed to "thick" or dense physical matter.</note> / just as this body is thick according to its substance. In early modern theology, spiritual substances like the soul were often described as "thin" or "subtle" compared to "thick" or "dense" physical matter.</note>, it contains within itself the eye through which it sees; the ear through which it hears; likewise also the tongue through which it speaks; and the hand; and remarkably the entire body. And since the soul maintains these members of the body, it is tempered therewith, and through them performs all the works of this life. In the very same way, God—who is infinite and cannot be grasped by the understanding—has humbled Himself through His goodness, and has put on the members of this body, and has withheld Himself from that unapproachable glory; and through kindness and friendliness, changing His form <note>original: "figure"</note>, He has made Himself bodily. ### On the Advancement in the Christian Life. ###

### Homily IV. 29 ###

...if gold or silver is thrown into the fire, its hard nature is undone and it is changed into a malleable state <note>original: "mowere." This refers to the softened, workable state of metal when heated.</note>: and the longer they remain in the fire, the more they are dissolved and changed from their natural hardness through the warm power of the fire.

So also the soul that has forsaken the world, desiring only the Lord, and with all searching of the spirit, with all diligence and labor, and out of its faith and hope, has placed a constant expectation upon Him—having received that heavenly fire of divine and spiritual love—is then truly released from the world. It is delivered from all the fragility of the passions <note>original: "affectien." In this theological context, these are worldly attachments or emotional inclinations that draw the soul away from God.</note> and drives all things away from itself, after its natural condition and the hardness of sins is changed, regarding all other things as superfluous, and resting in the heavenly bridegroom alone, whom it has received, through His fiery and unspeakable love.

I say to you: if these God-loving brothers—whom God Himself also has before His eyes—withdraw themselves from that love through obstacles, then they faint, so to speak. For the soul's life and rest is the spiritual and hidden participation in the heavenly kingdom.

For if the love of fleshly marriage separates a man from father, mother, and brothers, so that all these things are to the heart as external things; and if the person loves them, he loves them as external: but sets all his heart and mind upon his companion. Therefore, he says, shall a man leave father and mother, and shall cling to his wife: and they shall be two in one flesh. <margin>Matthew 19:5; Ephesians 5:31</margin> If then the love of the flesh thus releases the man from all other love, how much more shall those be released from all love of the world who are held worthy to be truly partakers of the holy, heavenly, and lovely Spirit? In this same manner also the people of Israel, when they (as mentioned before) had frequently sinned and trespassed, by killing God's prophets and committing many other wickednesses—for God, while remaining silent, exercised great long-suffering, enduring them for the sake of penance—they finally sinned so heavily that they were destroyed in such a way that they could no longer rise again, because they laid hands on their Lord God. <header>Homily IV.</header>

...and having thus acquired through His benefit the heavenly gift of faith, [the soul] places all her desire through grace in certain hope, being diligent so that she might serve the Holy Spirit suitably and fittingly for the remaining time of her life: daily advancing in that which is good, and remains firmly in the way of righteousness: staying steadfast until the end, and is in no way inclined toward vices: and by no means grieving the grace, earns eternal salvation with all the Saints: for as their companion and peer, while dwelling in the world, she followed them.

### How much the life of Christians differs from the life of other people.

### Homily 5

...are: and are obtained with labor, with sweat, with trials <note>original: "tentacien"; in this context, it refers to both spiritual temptations and the testing of one’s faith.</note>, and with very many struggles: which comes entirely from divine grace. For all people have a desire to see the earthly King: and he who dwells in any royal city desires to behold only the form of the king, or the ornament and the beauty of the purple garment, and the shining splendor of the precious stones, the set coronation, with the illustrious authority of the law sitting by him <note>This likely refers to the king’s counselors or the personification of judicial power that surrounds a monarch in court.</note>: except only for those who, because they are spiritual, do not value all such things: for they have felt another glory, namely the heavenly and incorporeal, and are radiant with another unspeakable light, and are partakers of other riches, and enjoying as it were another spirit, they converse according to the **inward man** <term>inward man: a term used by St. Paul to describe the spiritual self or the soul as it is renewed by God, distinct from the physical body</term>.

For the people of this world, and those into whom the spirit of this world has been poured, desire above all things this alone: that they may behold the earthly King with all his ornament and glory. For just as among all other things which are seen, the condition of this man [the king] is considered more fortunate by all people: and how he alone, through the beholding of him, is deemed more illustrious and more pleasant by everyone: so that each thinks within himself, "Oh, if only someone would give me that glory, decoration, and brilliance": saying that he is blessed who is nevertheless just like him—who is earthly, and subject to the same passions, and also mortal—yet is desired for his perishable decoration and beauty. 

If it is so then, that fleshly people so greatly desire the glory of the earthly king: how much more do those whom the rays of the life-giving and divine spirit have enlightened, and whose hearts he has wounded <note>To be "wounded" by divine love is a common mystical metaphor describing a soul so overwhelmed by the love of God that it can think of nothing else.</note> with divine love for Christ the heavenly King, [who] are led into that form...

->How one should pray.<-

### HOMILY VI.

THOSE who come to the Lord must
pour out their prayers in silence, peace, and
great tranquility, without using
unbecoming and disturbed shouting: but
with the focus of the heart and with pure thoughts, they
should keep God before their eyes. Just as someone
who is seized by illness and is branded with a hot iron,
or is treated by a <term>surgeon</term> <note>original: "chirurgien"; in this period, surgeons performed physical operations like cauterization often without anesthesia.</note>, bears the pain
inflicted upon him strongly and patiently,
without clamor or commotion, restraining himself;
even though some others, suffering the same pain
when they feel the hot iron and are under the
surgeon’s hands, scream very inappropriately:
although the pain of the one who screams and
of the one who does not scream, of the one who
agitates himself and of the one who keeps himself
still, is exactly the same. Some, when they are thus
tormented and distressed, bear it with a more tranquil
heart and restrain the thoughts of their heart without
commotion. Others, feeling the same distress, when they
suffer it with an ill heart, pray to God with commotion
and disturbance: so that those who hear it are
offended and unsettled by it. There are yet others who,
even though they are not distressed by any pain,
nonetheless out of boastfulness or through habit,
use—

...use unbecoming shouting, thinking they might
be able to please God through this means. But a
servant of God ought in no way to maintain such a
disorderly manner, but to act meekly
<margin>Isaiah 66:2</margin> and wisely: Just as the Prophet says,

And in the time of Moses and of Elijah one can find
that in the revelations that occurred to them, many
services of trumpets and powers preceded the
majesty of the Lord: But the coming of the Lord
(all these things being set aside) is revealed
in peace, silence, and tranquility. For
<margin>1 Kings 19:12 <note>original: "3. Reg. 19. c"; in older Bibles, the books of Kings and Samuel were numbered differently.</note></margin> behold, he says, the voice of a thin breeze, and the
Lord was in it. So it appears then, that the rest
of the Lord lies in peace and silence. Whatever
foundation then a person lays, and whatever 
beginning they take, therein they remain until the 
end. If it is that they begin the start of their 
prayer with a high and complaining voice, they 
will keep that same habit until the end. But the 
Lord, who is full of all kindness, does not refuse 
help to these people. These same ones then, through

### Homily VI.

...others, as they cannot pray everywhere, neither in the churches, nor in the neighborhoods, but perhaps in the wilderness, if they so desire. But those who pray with silence and tranquility edify all people in all places. All of a person’s diligence must be applied to their thoughts: and the surrounding material of evil thoughts must be cut away: and the person must stretch themselves toward God, not being submissive to the will of the thoughts: but when he has gathered them from all sides, he must distinguish the natural from the evil. For the soul that is surrounded by sin is placed like a great forest on a mountain, or like reeds in a flood of water, or like a thick growth of thorns and rough brushwood: those who wish to pass through it must stretch out their hands, nor can they drive away the surrounding material without strength and labor. In nearly the same way, the material of thoughts proceeding from the power of the <term>adversaries</term> <note>original: "teghenpartijen"; likely referring to demonic forces or spiritual temptations that oppose the soul.</note> stands against the soul. Therefore, one must watch and be diligent with great industriousness of heart to distinguish these external thoughts, which are breathed in by the power of the adversary. For some people, trusting in their own power, let themselves think they can cast these approaching mountains to the earth. There is also another who, ruling their heart with tranquility and <term>discretion</term> <note>original: "bescheedenheydt"; in this context, it refers to modest wisdom and the ability to discern spiritual truths.</note>, completes their work with less labor and more speed than the aforementioned person. The same happens in prayer. One finds some who shout in a disorderly way: who, as if trusting in the power of the body, do not know the <term>thefts</term> <note>original: "dieuerijen"; a common spiritual metaphor for how idle or intrusive thoughts "steal" the mind's focus away from God.</note> of the thoughts: but believe they perform a perfect work through their own power. There are also others who pay heed to the thoughts and completely fulfill the inward struggle. And these, by means of their diligent observation and discernment, are able to...

### How one should pray.

We find in the Apostle <note>Referring to St. Paul.</note> that he who edifies others is called greater. For he says: *He who speaks with tongues edifies himself: but he who prophesies edifies the church.* For he who prophesies is greater than he who speaks with tongues. Let everyone, then, rather choose to edify others: so shall he be counted worthy of the kingdom of heaven. **Question.** When some say that the Thrones and Crowns are creatures and not spirits, how is that to be understood?

**Answer.** Our spirit is the throne of the godhead: and conversely, the throne of our spirit is the godhead and the Holy Spirit. Likewise, Satan and similar <term>potentates</term> <note>original: "potentaten"; referring to high-ranking demonic authorities or "powers" mentioned in Pauline theology.</note> and princes of darkness have, from the time of the transgression of the commandment, sat in Adam’s heart, spirit, and body, as if in their own throne. For this reason, the Lord came and received a body from the Virgin. For had it pleased Him to come to us in pure and naked godhead, who would have been able to endure it? But He spoke to men through the organ or instrument of the body. Therefore, He has driven the spirits of wickedness, which troubled the body, out from the thrones of the understanding and of our thoughts where they frequented: and the Lord has purified the conscience: and the heart, and the thoughts, and the body He has arranged as a throne for Himself.

**Question.** What is it, then, that He said, <margin>Matthew 19:28</margin> *You shall sit upon the twelve seats, judging the twelve tribes of Israel?* **Answer.** We find that this was fulfilled upon the earth when the Lord ascended into heaven. For He sent into the twelve Apostles the Spirit, the Comforter, and the holy power, which, coming, dwelt and sat in the thrones

# Homily VII.

**Answer.** The Sun, which is a creature <note>original: "creature"; in this context, it refers to any created thing, not just a living being.</note> that shines in muddy places, is in no way defiled thereby: How much less is the divine power tainted or stained if it is near Satan. God has permitted evil in order to exercise <note>original: "oeffenen"; meaning to train, test, or discipline.</note> mankind: but this evil is darkened and blinded, and it cannot behold that which is pure and simple in God. If someone firmly asserts that Satan has his own place and that God also has His own, he confines Him <note>God</note> to the places where this wicked wretch <note>original: "boos-wicht"</note> lives: How then do we claim that this good is uncontainable and incomprehensible, and that all things are within it, and that good is not defiled by evil? Not at all. For considering that heaven, and the sun, and mountains are in God, and continue to stand through Him, are they also God? They are handiwork made to serve in their order: but the craftsman who is already present with His creatures is God. **Question.** Since wickedness transforms itself into an angel of light and resembles grace: how shall man recognize the footpaths of the devil? And how shall he embrace and distinguish the pathways of grace? **Answer.** Everything that proceeds from grace is endowed with joy, with peace, love, and truth: and the truth compels man to examine the truth: But everything that proceeds from wickedness is full of turmoil <note>original: "beroerten"; meaning agitation or disturbance.</note>, and has no love or joy toward God. Just as endive or white chicory <note>original: "cicoreye"</note> is like lettuce <note>original: "lattouwen"</note>: yet one is sweet and the other bitter, though they resemble each other: So also in grace there are some things like the truth; indeed, it <note>grace</note> is the very state of truth. Just as in the sun there is the radiance and the orb as well: and the radiance shows itself one way, and the light, which

### Of the Loving-Kindness of Christ.

Likewise, the radiance of a lamp that gives clarity in a house and shines everywhere is different from the light itself, which is clearer and more pleasant within the lamp: in this same way, some things come from grace which man views from afar like visions, and he rejoices in such visions and is transformed: because the divine power enters into him, which sustains his limbs and takes his heart and understanding captive in the love of God. Just as Peter, when he was captured and cast into prison, and the angel came into the locked prison and broke his bonds and brought him out from there: he was as if out of his mind <note>original: "buyten sijnen verstande"; meaning in a state of ecstasy or trance-like wonder.</note>, and thought that it was a vision. **Question.** But how do those fall who are governed by grace? **Answer.** The thoughts which are pure <note>original: "supuer"; an archaic spelling of *zuiver*, meaning pure or clean.</note> by their nature can slip and fall. For the man who begins to be exalted, considering himself righteous, condemns another and says, "You are a sinner." Truly, you do not know what Saint Paul said:

There was given to me a thorn in the flesh, a messenger of Satan <note>original: "Satans enghel"; literally "Satan's angel," referring to the "messenger of Satan" mentioned in 2 Cor 12:7.</note>, to buffet me on my cheeks, so that I should not exalt myself. For some take away revelation and say that the vision occurs through knowledge and sense. **Answer.** There is sense, vision, and also enlightenment. The enlightened one is better than he who only has sense: for his understanding is enlightened, and he has obtained an exceedingly better portion than he who only has sense: considering that he sees within himself a certainty of visions. But revelation is another thing, through which very great matters and divine mysteries <note>original: "godtlicke verholentheden"; meaning hidden divine secrets or sacred mysteries.</note> are revealed to the soul.

# Homily VII.

**Question.** Is it possible that someone may see the soul through the benefit of divine revelation and enlightenment? **Answer.** Just as these eyes behold the sun, so do those who are enlightened see the image of the soul. But few Christian people see this. **Question.** Does the soul have a form? **Answer.** Certainly, it has an image and form resembling the likeness of an angel. Just as the angels have form and image, and just as the outer man has an image, so the inner man has an image, being like the angel and the outer man. **Question.** Does the <term>discerning heart</term> <note>original: "verstandich herte"; referring to the seat of spiritual intellect and perception.</note> differ from the soul? **Answer.** Just as the members of the body, though they are many, are yet called "one man," so also are the members of the soul many: namely, the discerning heart, the conscience, the will, and the accusing and excusing thoughts. All these are bound together in one reason, but are nonetheless members of the soul. But the soul is one—namely, the inner man. And just as the outer eyes see from afar the thorns, ditches, and dangerous steep places, so also the discerning heart, since it is more transparent, foresees from afar the cunning and snares of the opposing power, and protects the soul as its eyes. Let us give glory to the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit, for ever and ever. AMEN.

# Concerning the occurrences in prayer.

It happens that one who has been occupied with other things throughout the whole day gives himself to an hour of prayer; and his inner man is taken up into the progress of prayer and into the depths of that eternity, with great sweetness: so that his spirit, being entirely caught up, wanders in strange places; and at that same time, all thoughts of earthly care are forgotten, because the thoughts are occupied and captured by divine and heavenly things, which are infinite and incomprehensible: yes, even in some wonderful things that cannot be expressed with human mouths; so that at that very moment he prays, saying: "Oh, if only the soul would fly away together with the prayer!" **Question.** Does a person go so deep into such things at all times, whenever they please? **Answer.** Grace is continually with him, rooted and mingled from the beginning of life <note>original: "beghinsel des leuens"; this refers to the start of the spiritual life or regeneration.</note>, like a natural and congealed thing which is in man like a <term>substance</term> <note>original: "substancie"; used here in a philosophical sense to mean an underlying essence or reality.</note>; but it rules man in various ways according to its pleasure, as it is most profitable. For sometimes the fire burns and ignites violently, and sometimes weakly and faintly. The light also sometimes becomes more sparked and luminous; sometimes it becomes smaller and gloomier. Thus also this lamp—namely, grace—is always burning and shining; when it shines more brightly, it is more ignited by the <term>drunkenness of God's love</term> <note>original: "dronckenschap der liefden Godts"; a mystical term for spiritual ecstasy or being overwhelmed by divine love.</note>; and again, it also shines with tempering <note>original: "verhandelinghe"; here likely referring to a moderation or withdrawal of intensity.</note>, and the light within it becomes dimmer. For this reason, the **perfect state** <term>perfecten staet: a state of complete spiritual maturity or sinlessness</term> is not granted to man permanently: so that he might be able to work diligently and care for his brothers, and for the **ministry of the word**. Nevertheless, that middle path or wall which blocked the way has been opened, and death is overcome. This is how the matter stands: Just as a dark power subtly seizes and covers the lamp that always burns and shines with a thick mist, so too has a covering been placed over this light. Therefore, this man confesses that he is not **perfect** and entirely free from sin; yet he dares to say that the middle path that blocked the way has been destroyed and dug through, while in another sense it is not yet fully opened, at least not at all times. For sometimes **grace** <term>gratie: divine favor or influence acting in the soul</term> ignites a person more fully, comforting and preparing him; but at other times it is withdrawn and diminished, according to the measure of God’s **dispensation** <note>original: "dispensatie"; the divine plan or arrangement of events and how God provides for the soul's needs</note> and according to what is beneficial for the person.

But who has reached that perfect degree and has tasted and tested that age to come? For I have not yet seen a perfect or free Christian man. Even if someone rests in grace and attains hidden mysteries and revelations, and to the great sweetness of grace, he nevertheless still has sin within him. But those who are in such a state, because of the very great grace and the light which they have received, believe they are free and perfect. Though they possess the power of grace, they are nonetheless deceived, being inexperienced men. I have not yet seen anyone who is truly free. For I myself have sometimes reached that degree in part, and through learning I have understood that no man is perfect.

In **degrees** grace works thus: It stills all the **members** <term>leden: the physical limbs or senses of the body</term> and the heart as well: so that the soul, through the very great joy, seems to be like a little child that knows no evil: nor does the man henceforth condemn any Greek or Jew, sinner or **man of the world** <note>original: "wereldts plegher"; one who is occupied with worldly affairs rather than spiritual ones</note>: but the **inner man** <term>inwendighe mensch: a Pauline concept referring to the spiritual self renewed by God</term> looks upon all people with a pure eye: and rejoices in the whole world: and desires to honor and love the Greeks as well as the Jews. At another time, like a king’s son, he trusts the Son of God as his father: and the door is opened to him, through which he enters into many **dwellings** <note>A reference to the "many mansions" in the Father's house mentioned in John 14:2</note>: and as he goes further inward, in proportion there are opened to him beyond the hundred dwellings, yet another hundred: and then he becomes rich. And as he becomes richer, again other newer wonders are shown to him. To him, as a son and heir, are entrusted things that human nature cannot **comprehend** <note>original: "gheleggen"; to grasp, explain, or lay out in words</note>, nor mouth or tongue express. Glory be to God.

That the divine promises and prophecies are fulfilled through manifold progress and **temptations** <note>original: "becoringhen"; trials or tests of faith</note>: And that we are delivered from the **assaults** <note>original: "aenvechtingen"; spiritual attacks or buffetings</note> of the devils, by clinging to God alone.

# Of the divine promises and deliverances. #

[the work] of grace is visibly perfected, as one's free will is shown through manifold **temptations** <term>tentatien: trials or tests of faith</term> to please the Holy Spirit: and reveals, as time progresses, the proof and patience. We shall set before our eyes this consequence through visible examples from the scriptures inspired by God. That which I say, 
<margin>Joseph.</margin> 
is just as is said of Joseph. The will of God ordained beforehand for Joseph—after how long a time was it put to work, and were the dreams fulfilled? With how much labor, **suffering** <note>original: "quellaedsien"; torment, hardship, or affliction</note> and anxieties was he first tested: and he bore it all manfully, and was tested in all things and found to be a faithful servant of God? Then he became king of Egypt: and sustained his family: and the prophecy of that which was not seen, and the will of God which was announced through many times and **ordinances** <note>original: "bestellinghen"; divine arrangements or appointments</note> beforehand, was fulfilled.
<margin>David.</margin>
The same is also written of David, whom God anointed to be a king through the ministry of Samuel the prophet: Immediately after he was anointed, he had to flee because of the persecution that was approaching him from Saul, who wished to bring him to death. Where then was the divine anointing? Where was the promise, which it seemed would be fulfilled immediately? For shortly after the anointing he was heavily tormented, departing into the wilderness and having a lack of bread; he immediately took flight to foreign nations, on account of the great cunning and **snares** <note>original: "laghen"; traps or ambushes</note> that were set by Saul against him whom God had anointed as King. Such sufferings he nevertheless bore patiently: thereafter, through a long span of time, he was tested, plagued, and greatly **tried** <term>ghetenteert: put to the test, specifically regarding one's faith or resolve</term>, and bore it with long-suffering, for he believed in God especially,

<summary>This page uses the biblical stories of Joseph and David to illustrate that God’s promises and spiritual perfection are realized only after long periods of suffering, testing, and patient endurance.</summary>

### Homily IX. ###

and certainly allowed himself to be convinced, saying: "That which God has made known through the **anointing** <note>original: "salvinghe"; the ritual act of pouring oil to consecrate a king</note> performed upon me by the prophet, and that which He has said before would happen to me, that same must without doubt be fulfilled." And at last, after long waiting, God’s will was fulfilled, and David reigned after many **temptations** <term>tentacien: trials or tests of faith</term>. And then God’s word became clear and manifest, and the anointing performed by the prophet was shown to be certain and true.

<margin>Moses.</margin>
It fared the same way with Moses. When God had previously known and ordained him to be a future leader and **redeemer** <note>original: "verlosser"</note> of the people, He brought it about that he was accepted by Pharaoh’s daughter as her own child. Who, being increased with royal riches, with glory and pleasures, was instructed in all the teachings of the Egyptians: and when he had come to man’s estate, he renounced it all together, preferring **tribulation** <note>original: "tribulatie"; hardship or affliction</note> and reproach for **Christ's** <note>original: "Christus"; the author views Moses’ suffering for his people as a type or precursor of suffering for Christ</note> sake, according to the word of the Apostle, than the temporary enjoyment of sin.

<margin>Hebrews 11:24</margin>
And when he had fled from Egypt: for how long a time was he a shepherd—he who was the son of a king, and had been raised in all sweetness and pleasure? After that, this same man was tested by God, and was found faithful through much patience: after he had suffered many **temptations** <term>tentatien: trials or tests of faith</term> beforehand: and was later made redeemer, ruler, and king of Israel: indeed, he was even named **Pharaoh’s god** <note>original: "Pharaons godt"; a reference to Exodus 7:1, where God tells Moses he will be as a god to Pharaoh in terms of power</note> by God Himself. Through this man, God struck the land of Egypt, and showed very great and wonderful things through his ministry against the mighty Pharaoh: and at last drowned the Egyptians in the sea. See here, after how long a time God’s will and His purpose were made clear: and after how great trials and great **torments** <note>original: "quellinghen"; distress or sufferings</note> it was fulfilled in deed?

# Of the Divine Promises and Redemptions #

<margin>Abraham</margin>
The same is also written of **Abraham**. For how long a time before had God promised to give him a son? Yet He did not give him one immediately; meanwhile, in the intervening space of many years, he fell into many **trials** <term>versoeckingen: tests of faith or character</term> and temptations. But by enduring everything that happened to him with **long-suffering** <note>original: "lanckmoedichlick"; a patient endurance despite hardship</note>, he strengthened himself in faith, knowing that He who had made the promise was not a liar, but that He would keep and fulfill His word. Thus he was found faithful and obtained that which had been promised to him.

<margin>Noah</margin>
In the same way, consider **Noah**, when he was commanded by God in his five-hundredth year to build an ark; for God had promised to send the **Great Flood** <note>original: "sundt-vloedt"</note> upon the earth. And when God finally allowed this to come in the six-hundredth year, Noah had waited for one hundred years with long-suffering, never doubting whether God would do what He had promised or not. Instead, he remained certain that the things God had spoken must undoubtedly come to pass. Thus he was found upright in will, faith, patience, and long-suffering, and was saved alone with his family, because he had diligently kept God's commandment. 

These examples from the scriptures have been brought forward to show that the power of **divine grace** <note>original: "godtlicke gratie"</note> given to man, and the gift of the Holy Spirit—which the faithful soul deserves to receive—is obtained through great effort, patience, and long-suffering, amidst **temptations** <term>tentacien: trials or tests</term> and trials. This is while the **free will** <note>original: "vrijen wille"</note> is tested by all manner of afflictions. If the soul does not grieve the Holy Spirit in any way, but aligns with grace in all commandments, it then deserves to receive **redemption** <note>original: "verlossinghe"</note> from its miseries, and fully attains the election of the spirit, which used to be spoken of in the **mystery** <note>original: "mysterie"; likely referring to the hidden or spiritual meaning of the sacraments or divine plan</note>; and the spiritual rich—

<summary>This page uses the biblical examples of Abraham and Noah to illustrate that divine promises are fulfilled only after long periods of testing and patient endurance. It concludes by arguing that the gift of the Holy Spirit and spiritual redemption are earned through the cooperation of free will with divine grace during times of trial.</summary>

<keywords>Abraham, Noah, Great Flood, divine grace, free will, patience, long-suffering, trials, Holy Spirit, redemption</keywords>

<margin>Proud souls.</margin>
For when they had obtained the grace of the spirit, and the consolation of grace, with pleasure, delight, and spiritual sweetness: they began, trusting therein, to be puffed up and to act negligently, not being sorrowful of heart or humbled in spirit: nor did they come to the perfect degree of freedom <term>ledicheydt: literally "emptiness," here meaning a state where the heart is cleared of worldly distractions</term> from the passions <term>affectien: the disordered desires or emotional attachments that lead to sin</term>, or to the upright fullness of grace with all diligence and faith: but being forsaken and self-satisfied, they remained in a small consolation of grace: so that such souls have progressed and increased more in pride than in humility: and are thus entirely deprived of that gift of grace which they had obtained, on account of the unholy contempt and the vain pride of their intentions.
