For this reason one must work and struggle greatly: for it is not fitting that the bridegroom who comes for your sake should be beaten and crucified, and that the bride, for whose sake he comes, should glory and be distracted <note>original: *afghetrocken* — often used in mystical texts to describe being drawn away from God by worldly concerns or spiritual pride.</note>. For just as in things set before our eyes, if a harlot shamefully gives herself over to all men: so also the soul that gives itself over to all devils is violated by these same spirits. For there are some who are burdened with sin and vice with a deliberate will, but some without their will. What is this? Those are vicious with a deliberate will who have given over their own will to vice, who rejoice therein, and make friendship with it. Such people have peace with Satan, nor do they wage any war in their thoughts against the devil. 

But those who are such without their will: these have sin fighting against them in their members, according to the Apostle <note>Referring to St. Paul in Romans 7:23, "I see another law in my members, warring against the law of my mind."</note>: and the misty power and darkness is against their desire: nonetheless they do not consent in their thoughts, nor do they have any pleasure in them, nor are they obedient to them, but speak and act against them, and become angry with themselves: who are much better and more worthy before God than those who, by their own counsel, give their own will to vice and rejoice in it. <meta>This page continues the discussion of free will and grace from page 203. # Of the dignity and state of man.

Some men, being powerful in dignity, wealth, and nobility, by their own design and will, leave their wealth, nobility, and dignity behind, and go away, putting on filthy and base clothing, and choosing contempt instead of glory, and becoming wretched and cast out; this is placed within their own will. But I say to you, that even the Apostles, who were perfected in <term>grace</term> <note>original: *gratie* — the unmerited favor and divine influence of God.</note>, were not hindered or turned away by that grace from doing what they wished, even if they themselves had wanted to do that which displeased grace. For our nature contains both good and evil; and the opposing power <note>Referring to the devil or the inclination toward sin.</note> has the strength to exhort, but not to compel. Furthermore, in other matters, you have your free will to incline yourself toward whichever side you wish. Do you not see that Peter was rebuked, and that Paul, while departing, punished him <note>A reference to the Incident at Antioch (Galatians 2:11), where Paul publicly confronted Peter for his inconsistent behavior regarding Jewish dietary laws.</note>? Although he was such a great man, he was nevertheless still subject to correction. Paul also, although he was spiritual, had a dispute by his own will against Barnabas; and being embittered, they separated from one another.
<margin>Galatians 6:1</margin> Thus says the same Paul: You who are spiritual, instruct such people in the spirit of meekness; considering yourself, so that you also are not tempted. See, the spiritual are tempted; for the freedom of the will is still there, and the enemies still press closely as long as they are in this world. 
<margin>Whether the Apostles were able to sin.</margin> **Question.** Could the Apostles not sin if they had wanted to? Or was grace more powerful than the will?
**Answer.** They could not sin, for they were not puffed up <note>original: *verheuuen* — to be raised up in pride or arrogance.</note>, because they were endowed with such light and grace. Nevertheless, we do not say that grace in them was almighty <note>In this theological context, "almighty" means irresistible; the author argues that grace does not force a person to be good against their will.</note>; but we say that grace also permits the spiritual and perfect to have their will, and the power to do what they desire,

<meta>catchword: appetite/desire</meta>

<summary>This page argues that divine grace does not override human free will, using the disagreements between Apostles like Paul, Peter, and Barnabas as evidence that even the "spiritual" are subject to temptation and error. It concludes that while the Apostles did not sin because they were filled with light, grace remained a cooperative force rather than a compulsory one.</summary>

### Homily XXVII.

desire and to incline toward whatever they wish. Yes, even human nature—though it is the lowliest of all—even if there is good within it, it nonetheless possesses an <term>unstable power</term> <note>original: *wanckelbaere macht* — referring to the human capacity to fluctuate or change between good and evil; a core concept of free will.</note>. It is just as if some men wore all kinds of armor, breastplates, and other body-protections, and were further protected from within, and yet the enemies did not attack them with force. Or even if they were attacked, it was placed within their own will whether to use the weapons, to fight against the enemies, and to achieve victory; or to enjoy themselves with the enemies and make peace, and not to fight, even though they possess the weapons.

So it is also with Christian people: though they are clothed with perfect strength and possess the heavenly weapons, if they so wish, they may delight themselves with Satan and make peace with him without fighting. For nature is unstable; if a person wishes, he becomes a son of God; or if he wishes otherwise, he becomes a <term>son of perdition</term> <note>original: *sone der verderffenissen* — a biblical term (John 17:12) referring to one destined for spiritual destruction</note>, because <term>free will</term> <note>original: *vryen wille*</note> remains evident. 

It is one thing to speak of bread and the table, and it is another to eat and enjoy the sweetness of the bread, so that all the limbs are strengthened thereby. It is one thing to speak only with words of a very sweet drink, and it is another to go and take from the fountain and be filled with the taste of that very sweet drink. It is one thing to speak of war and of brave and valiant fighters, and it is another to march in battle array and go to work against the enemy—going out, coming in, taking, giving, and achieving the victory. 

Thus it is also in spiritual matters. It is one thing to explain sermons with knowledge and understanding, and it is another—in substance, in action, in assurance, and in the inner man and the heart—to possess the treasure, the grace, the taste, and the power of the Holy Spirit. ### On the dignity and state of man.

God or not: if it is not so, then tell me the obstacle. Truly Satan and the devils are evil spirits, who burden the heart and find the soul. For the cunning devil has crafty tricks, double gates, and many deceits: he possesses the paths and the thoughts of the soul, nor does he allow one to pray sincerely and come to God. For nature can be a partaker of the devils and evil spirits, and also of the angels and the Holy Spirit: she is the temple of Satan, and the temple of the Holy Spirit. Therefore, brothers, you must well perceive and consider whose partaker you are, of the angels or of the devils: whose temple and dwelling you are, God’s or the Devil’s: with what treasure the heart is full, of grace or of Satan. Therefore it must be completely purified, like a house full of stench and filth; it must be decorated and filled with all good scents and treasures: so that the Holy Spirit may come before Satan,
<margin>The Word without works does not make a Christian person.</margin>
and rest in the souls of the Christians. But when someone has heard the Word, he does not immediately belong to the good party. For if he were to belong to the good party immediately upon hearing, there would have been no struggles, or causes of wars, or wanderings <note>The author uses "loopinghen" (runnings/wanderings) to describe the arduous journey and effort required in the spiritual life.</note>: but as soon as he had heard it, he would have come to perfect rest and measure. This is not how it is arranged. For if you say that, you take away man’s will, and you deny that there is a conflicting power that wrestles with the soul. But we say that he who hears the Word comes to a movement of the heart: thereafter, as grace withdraws by dispensation <note>original: *dispensatie* — here meaning God's purposeful management or temporary withholding of felt grace to test the believer.</note> for the benefit of man, he comes to knightly <term>spiritual exercise</term> <note>original: *ruyterlicke oeffeninghe* — literally "knightly practice," suggesting the soul must act like a brave soldier or knight in combat.</note> and instruction: furthermore he wrestles and fights against Satan, and after he has achieved victory through much running and fighting, then he becomes a Christian man. # Homily XXVII.

...were it that someone belonged to the good party solely through hearing, without labor: then also those who take pleasure in spectacles and fornicators would likewise enter into the kingdom and into life, which truly no one shall give them without labor and struggle: for the way is narrow and full of distress. One must go through this hard way, suffer, and be tormented <note>The Dutch "ghepijnelt" suggests both physical torture and the internal "pains" of spiritual discipline.</note>, and thus come into life. For if one could proceed happily without labor, then Christianity would no longer be the stone of stumbling or of offenses <note>original: "steẽ des aenstoots/ oft der ergernissen"; a reference to 1 Peter 2:8, describing Christ as a stumbling block to those who do not believe.</note>: nor would there be faith and unbelief. 

Furthermore, <margin>Free will.</margin> he also places man unchangeably toward the good and toward the evil. For to him who can be changed to either side, and who has free will, a law is given, that he might wage war against the opposing power. For to the fixed and unchangeable nature, no law is set. Sun, Heaven, and earth are held by no law: for they are creatures of nature that is firmly bound: and are therefore not subject to reward or punishment. 

For honor and glory are prepared for that which may be converted to good: but on the contrary, pain and punishment are also prepared for that same changeable nature, which can shun the evil party and extend toward the good and right. See, you say that he is of a bound nature. Furthermore, you do not make the good man praiseworthy. For a simple-hearted <note>original: "slechtmoedich," meaning one who is naturally docile or lacks a strong, conflicting spirit.</note> and good person by nature is not worthy of praise, even if he is desired. For that which is not good by design <note>original: "wt opset," meaning by intentional choice or purpose.</note> is not praiseworthy, even if it is pleasant. For that person is worthy of praise who, having free will, embraces that which is good by his own will, not without struggle and warfare.

### Of the dignity and state of man.

...struggled: so also the opposing power and the soul have equal capability within themselves; and Satan has equal power to admonish and lure the soul to his will, and the soul, on the other hand, has the power to resist and not be obedient in any way. For both powers may be exhorted toward good and toward evil, yet they are not necessarily compelled. To this will is given divine reward <note>original: "vergheldinghe"; implies a just recompense or consequence for the choices made by free will</note>, and in the struggle, it may take up weapons from heaven, with which it shall root out and overcome sin. For the soul may renounce sin, but it cannot overcome and drive out vice without God's help. Those who say that sin is like a mighty giant <note>original: "dapper ruese"</note> and the soul like a small child do not speak well. For if matters were so unequal—that sin were the giant and the soul the child—then the law-giver would be unjust, having ordained that man shall struggle against Satan. But the foundation of the way that leads to God is this: namely, to complete the way of life in great patience, in hope, in humility, in poverty of spirit, and in gentleness. And through these things, a man may obtain righteousness within himself; but we call the Lord Himself "Righteousness." For these commandments which thus command are like fixed milestones and signs of the royal way, through which the traveler is led to the <margin>Matthew 5:a</margin> heavenly city. For He says: Blessed are the poor in spirit; blessed are the gentle; blessed are the peacemakers. Say that this is Christianity. If anyone does not walk by this way, he goes wandering through false paths and uses an evil foundation. Glory be to the mercies of the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit for eternity. AMEN.

# Description and lamentation of the wretched souls, in which Christ does not dwell because of sin.

### H O M I L Y XXVIII.

<margin>Jerusalem destroyed</margin> Just as God in times past, being angry with the Jews, gave Jerusalem over to their enemies as a public spectacle, and those who hated them ruled over them; and there was no longer any feast day nor sacrifice: So also God, being angered at the soul because of the transgression of the commandment, has given it over to its enemies—to the devils and to the passions <note>original: "affectien"; in this context, it refers to disordered worldly desires or passions that cloud the soul's judgment</note>. When these had led the soul astray, they utterly destroyed it; no feast day was held there anymore, nor was incense nor any offering made to God. Instead, in the pathways of the soul, there were signs of horrible beasts, and the creeping spirits unleashed their wickedness within it. And just as a house, unless it has a master living within it, becomes dark, vile, and shameful, and becomes full of filth and dung: So also the soul that does not have its Lord within it, rejoicing with the angels, becomes filled with the darkness of sins, with the filth of the passions, and with every kind of shame. Woe to the road where no one walks, nor is a human voice heard: for it is the haunt of beasts. Woe to the soul, when the Lord does not enter into it, so that He might drive out with His voice the spiritual beasts of wickedness. Woe to the house where the Lord does not dwell. Woe to the earth if it has no husbandman <note>original: "lantwinner"; an archaic term for a farmer or tiller of the soil</note> to cultivate it. Woe to the ship that has no pilot <note>original: "stierman"; literally a steersman</note>: for it perishes when it is driven by the floods and tempests of the sea. Woe to the soul, if it does not have within it Christ the true pilot: for it is tossed about in the very rough

You find that they sail across the sea of evil powers and that they lead the faithful souls. You find that they are husbandmen <term>husbandmen: a traditional term for farmers or those who cultivate the land, here used metaphorically for spiritual growth</term> cultivating the vineyard of souls: you find that they are <margin>2 Corinthians 11:2</margin> bridal companions promising the souls to Christ in marriage. This, then, is that "least one" who is greater than John the Baptist. For just as the plowman, guiding a yoke of oxen, tills the land: so also our Lord Jesus, who is the preeminent and true husbandman, has sent out the Apostles two by two, cultivating with them the land of those who hear and truly believe. But it is also worth recounting that the kingdom of God and the preaching of the Apostles does not consist solely in the word heard—as if an eloquent man made a speech to others—but the kingdom is situated in the strength and power of the Spirit. For these things happened to the children of Israel, who constantly handled the Scriptures as if they were meditating attentively on the Lord; but because they did not receive the truth, they handed that same inheritance over to others. God bestows His grace in two ways: and He demands the fruits thereof back with a righteous judgment.

### HOMILY XXIX.

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut drop cap 'M' decorated with scrolling vine and floral patterns.</image-desc>Because the wisdom of God is infinite and cannot be comprehended, He carries out the <term>dispensations: the divine distribution or management of grace and trials to humanity</term> of grace toward the human race in an incomprehensible and unsearchable manner, in order to demonstrate man’s free will; so that those who love God with their whole heart and suffer all peril and labor for His sake might be made manifest. For those who are possessed by the grace and gift of the Holy Spirit—as soon as they have come to it through faith and prayer—sometimes turn away from the world without labor, sweat, or exhaustion. Furthermore, God does not give this grace idly, lightly, or by chance: but through a hidden and incomprehensible wisdom to test the desire and the free will of those who immediately receive the divine grace: whether they have felt the benefit, kindness, and sweetness shown to them by God, in proportion or similarity to the grace given to them without their own labor. When they are then deemed worthy of this, they must show diligence, haste, labor, and fruit coming from their own will and intent, and make a repayment for the gifts; that is, they must place themselves entirely and inwardly toward the love of the Lord, doing His will alone, and turning away completely from all fleshly desires. But that God [gives grace to] those who have separated themselves from the world and, according to the <term>sentence of the Gospel: the teachings or declarations found in the New Testament</term>, have renounced—

### Of the grace of God, and the fruits thereof.

...[received] <note>The text begins mid-word, likely continuing from "ontvangen" (received) or a similar verb on the previous page.</note> nor have they fully felt it, because of the secret wisdom and the unspeakable judgments of God, who in various ways tests the believing souls and diligently notes the love that comes from the will and from the intention. For there are certain boundaries, measures, and weights for the free intention, and for the willing love and diligence, according to what is given, toward all His holy commandments. Thus, those souls who fulfill the measure of their love and of their service deserve the kingdom and eternal life. For God is righteous, and His judgments are just, nor is there any partiality <note>original: "wtneminghe der persoonen," a biblical phrase meaning God does not show favoritism based on status.</note> with Him: but according to the measure of the granted benefits, both of the body and of the spirit, whether of knowledge, of understanding, or of discernment

(which God has poured into human nature in various ways), so shall He judge each one, and demand the fruits of virtues, and according to their worthiness, He shall reward each according to their works: and the mighty shall suffer pain proportionally <note>original: "matichlick," meaning in proportion to their responsibility. While some translations of Wisdom 6:6 suggest the mighty are "mightily" punished, this Dutch text emphasizes that the punishment is measured specifically against their greater power.</note>.

### Homily XXIX. 221

...day of judgment. For from everyone, according to what he knows, a reciprocal restitution <note>original: "restitutie," meaning a spiritual repayment or accounting of what was entrusted to the soul.</note> shall be demanded of the fruit of faith, of love, and of all virtue that has been given to him, whether he has received it through hearing, or whether he has never heard God’s word. For the faithful soul who loves the truth, and diligently observes the eternal goods that are laid up in keeping for the righteous, and the unspeakable benefit of the coming divine grace: she considers herself, her diligence, labor, and weariness <note>original: "vermoedtheydt," likely a variant of "vermoeidheid" meaning exhaustion or the weight of effort.</note> unworthy of the 
<margin>Poverty of spirit</margin> hidden promises of the Spirit. This is the one who is poor in spirit, whom the Lord calls blessed: this one hungers and thirsts after righteousness: this one is completely broken of heart. Those who take upon themselves such a purpose, diligence, labor, and desire for virtue, and remain therein until the end, shall truly be able to obtain the eternal life and kingdom. Therefore, let none of the brothers be exalted against his brother, and let him not be misled by vice, and not sin through a high esteem of himself, saying: "Behold, I have obtained the spiritual gift." It does not befit Christian people to think this in their heart. For you do not know what tomorrow shall bring to him: nor do you know what his end or your own shall be. But let each one observe himself well, and search his own conscience <gloss>or inward witness</gloss> without ceasing, and test the work of his heart, with what diligence and power his heart is set toward God: and that he, diligently observing the perfect goal of freedom, of the <term>emptiness of passions</term> <note>original: "ydelheydt van affectien," referring to the state of being free from worldly or sinful emotional attachments.</note> and of spiritual rest, may run without ceasing or laziness, and place his trust in no gift or justification. Glory and worship be to the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit, into eternity. AMEN.

### HOMILY XXX.

<margin>The word of God is not empty, but working powerfully.</margin>

Those who hear the word must demonstrate the work of the word in their own souls. For God’s word is not empty, but it has a work which is perfected in the soul: and it is for this reason called "work," <note>The author uses "work" (werck) to mean both the act of God and the resulting transformation or "effect" within the believer.</note> so that the work might also be found within those who hear it. So then the Lord gives the work of truth to the hearers, so that the word might be found fruitful in us. For just as the shadow goes before the body, and the shadow reveals the body, but the truth is the body itself: so also is the word like the shadow of the truth of Christ: but the word precedes the truth. 

Fathers living on the earth generate children from their own nature, from their own body and soul: and when these are born, they instruct them diligently with all their power, as their sons, until they become mature men, and successors and heirs. For all the purpose and all the diligence of fathers is situated in this: that they obtain children and have heirs. If it happens that they gain none, they are very sorrowful and grieved; and on the contrary, they are very glad when they have children; and the kinsmen and neighbors also rejoice because of it. 

In the same way, our Lord Jesus Christ, being careful for human salvation <note>original: "salicheydt," which can mean both salvation and the state of being blessed.</note>, has from the beginning fulfilled every arrangement and diligence through the service of the fathers, the patriarchs, the law, and the prophets. At the very last, coming Himself and despising the shame of the cross, He suffered death: and all His labor and diligence was situated in this: that He, from

<meta>catchword: Himself /</meta>

### HOMILY XXXII.

<margin>Diversity of languages.</margin>

ages. Among the creeping creatures there is also great difference. And among the birds there are various bodies. <image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'G' at the beginning of the homily.</image-desc>

### Homily XXXIV.

...extensively <note>original: "breedtsamelick," meaning broadly or collectively without reserve</note> and without any shame. For they are all together one in Christ, and they all rest in one light. One shall be attentive and diligent toward the other, and meanwhile, they shall quickly shine in the truth, in the true contemplation <term>contemplation: a state of deep spiritual looking or mystical awareness</term> of the hidden light: with so many kinds of figures, and with so many and various divine clarities shall they see one another adorned. Each shall be amazed <note>original: "verbaest," here meaning struck with awe or astonishment</note> beholding the glory of the other and shall rejoice with an unspeakable joy. You see how the glory of God—which belongs to the hidden light of the eternal mysteries and the innumerable goods—cannot be expressed or understood with words. Just as in things that one sees with the eyes: No one can count the manifold plants, seeds, and flowers of the earth; no one can measure or know all the riches of the earth; nor can one know the number, the genus, and the variety of the birds that fly in the air; nor can we comprehend the greatness of the heavens, or the position and the course of the stars: So also the unspeakable and incomprehensible riches of the Christians cannot be recounted or expressed with words. For if these creatures are in such a great and incomprehensible number for men: how much more is He who created and made them? So then must man rejoice and be glad all the more for such great riches, and for such a great inheritance that is prepared for the Christians, which no one can relate or tell. But we must with all diligence and humility enter into the struggle <note>original: "strijdt," referring to the spiritual battle or ascetic life</note> of the Christians, so that we may thereby obtain those riches. For God himself is the inheritance 
<margin>Psalm 16:5 <note>The source cites Psalm 15.b, following the Vulgate numbering.</note></margin> 
and the portion of the Christians. For there it stands written: That the Lord is the portion of mine inheritance and of my cup. ->Of the old and the new Sabbath day.<-

### HOMILY XXXV.

In the shadow of the law given through Moses, God commanded that every person should observe the Sabbath day and do no work. But this signified the figure <term>figure: a symbolic representation or prophetic type used in the Bible to foreshadow future spiritual truths</term> and the shadow of the true Sabbath which is given to the soul by the Lord. For the soul that has merited to be delivered from impure, foul thoughts celebrates the true Sabbath and rests the true rest, being idle <note>original: "ledich," meaning free from worldly labor or empty of sinful distractions</note> and delivered from all works of darkness. For although they rested in the time of the law so that they might observe the figurative Sabbath day, nonetheless their souls were bound with vices and wickedness. 

But the true Sabbath is the true rest of the soul, which is void <note>original: "ydel," here meaning emptied of worldly noise or cleared of sin</note> and purified from the thoughts of Satan, and rests in the eternal rest and joy of the Lord. Just as He then commanded at that time that even the irrational animals should rest on the Sabbath day—that the ox should not be pulled under a necessary yoke, and that the donkey should not be burdened with loads (for the beasts also rested from heavy works)—so also the Lord, when He had come and had given the true and eternal Sabbath, has comforted and refreshed the soul that was burdened and laden with the packs of wickedness and impure thoughts. This soul necessarily committed evil works, as it was brought under the slavery of the punishable powers <note>original: "straffebaren," referring to demonic forces or sinful impulses that lead to judgment</note>; He has unburdened her from the unbearable loads, from the vain and foul thoughts, taking away...

<summary>This page introduces Homily XXXV, which interprets the Mosaic Sabbath law as a prophetic "shadow" of the spiritual rest found in Christ. It contrasts physical rest for animals and humans with the true soul-rest that comes from being purified of sinful thoughts and demonic influence.</summary>
<keywords>Sabbath, spiritual rest, Moses, Law of Moses, soul purification, figurative vs true, shadow and figure, deliverance from sin</keywords>

This is just like grain sown in the earth. From one heart, it brings forth many and various grains. And among the ears of grain, some are broader, some narrower; but they are all gathered together in one threshing floor and barn; and although they differ, yet from all of them one bread is baked. Or just as in a city there are a multitude of people; some are small children, some are men or youths; but they all drink water from the selfsame fountain, and all eat one bread, and all breathe the same wind <note>original: "windt," here referring to the air or the breath of life</note>. Just as lamps, too, some have two lights, and some seven, and in one place the power of the light is greater, and the light is different: So also those who are in the fire and in the light cannot be found in the darkness; yet they differ greatly among one another. In the same way as a father who has two sons; one a child, and the other a youth: the youth he sends into the cities and lands; but the small child he keeps with him always, for it can do nothing <note>meaning it cannot yet care for itself or work</note>. Glory be to God. AMEN.

### Of paradise and of the spiritual law.

that which he forgives therein, shall be forgiven him.

For the law speaks thus: In the midst of judgment, judgment; and in the midst of forgiveness, forgiveness. Thus then is love the fulfillment of the law. We have said, the first law: not that God has set two laws for man, but one alone: which by nature is spiritual through the power of the law, and knowing for those who forgive others their misdeeds, it gives a righteous sentence for them: but for those who always desire to be in a better condition <note>Likely referring to those who seek worldly status or self-righteousness over spiritual humility.</note>, it brings many punishments. For David says:
<margin>Psalm 17:c <note>Original: "Pfal. 17. c"; This follows the Vulgate numbering; in modern Bibles, this is Psalm 18:26.</note></margin>
> With the chosen you shall be chosen, and with the perverse you shall act perversely.

Therefore those who spiritually fulfill the law, and partake of <term>grace<note>original: "gratie"; the unmerited favor and divine influence of God acting in the soul.</note></term> according to measure, they love not only the benevolent, but also the spiteful and the persecuting: receiving spiritual love as a reward for the good—for the good, I say, not because they have forgiven the injuries and spitefulness, but because they have shown kindness to the souls of those who committed the spitefulness. For they have presented these to God, as if they had obtained <term>salvation<note>original: "salichepdt"; implies both being saved and a state of eternal bliss or happiness.</note></term> through them, as it is written:
<margin>Matthew 5:a</margin>
> Blessed are you when they curse you, and when they persecute you, and they say all evil against you, lying, for my sake.

The spiritual law has taught them to feel thus. For while they suffer patiently, and maintain spiritual <term>meekness<note>original: "saechtmoedichepdt"; a gentle, humble, and patient disposition.</note></term>, the Lord—beholding the patience of the mind besieged by war <note>Refers to the internal struggle against temptations or anger.</note>, and that love in no way slackens—breaks down the <term>middle wall<note>original: "middelen vveech"; a reference to Ephesians 2:14, the "middle wall of partition" that separates man from God or groups from each other.</note></term>, like a bulwark, and drives out the enmity entirely: so that they now possess love not as if it were forced upon them by power and bestowed therein, but it is as a help to them: for the Lord afterwards restrains the <term>swinging sword<note>original: "svvinghende svveert"; likely an allusion to the flaming sword guarding the way to the Tree of Life in Genesis 3:24.</note></term>, which moves the thoughts: and they enter into the innermost part behind the veil, where the Lord the forerunner has entered for us:

church with my understanding"? <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 14:19, where Paul discusses speaking intelligibly rather than in tongues.</note> **Answer.** The church is understood in two persons: now as the assembly of the faithful, and now as the ordering of the souls. When this is understood spiritually within the man, then the church is the whole assembly thereof <note>Meaning the soul itself is an "assembly" or "church" of spiritual faculties.</note>. But the five words are the virtues by which the whole man is encompassed and joined together; and these are distinguished in many ways. For just as he who speaks in the Lord encompasses all wisdom through five words, so also he who follows the Lord through five virtues builds up religion with all zeal. Which, since they are five encompassing the others, are: first, prayer; second, temperance; [then] mercy, poverty, and long-suffering <note>original: "lanckmoedicheydt"</note>—words fulfilled through desire and will. And these are the words of the soul which are spoken by the Lord and are heard in the heart. For the Lord works, and then the Spirit speaks in the understanding; and the more the heart is driven by desire, the more the Spirit reveals itself while working. And just as these virtues encompass the others, so they bring each other forth mutually. For if the first is taken away, then they are all taken away. Take away the second as well, and the preceding ones perish...

# Homily XXXVII.

...encompassing, and so forth. For how shall anyone pray, without being moved by the spirit? Scripture gives me testimony, which says: <margin>1 Corinthians 12:a</margin> "No one can say, 'Lord Jesus,' except in the Holy Spirit." But how shall the steadfast persevere without the help of prayer? And he who is not steadfast in all things, how shall he show mercy to the hungry or to those who have suffered injustice? But he who is not moved by compassion also does not suffer any poverty <note>In this context, the author likely means that without compassion, one cannot truly participate in the "poverty of spirit" or share in the sufferings of the needy.</note>. Wrath—whether it possesses wealth or not—is the foster-child of greed. But the soul that is powerful in virtues progresses in the church in this way: not because it has done something, but because it has willed and desired to do something. For the work of man does not make the man blessed <margin>Good works.</margin>, but rather the one who gives the power to work.

Therefore, if anyone suffers the wounds <term>wondteeckenen (marks of the Lord, often referring to the stigmata or signs of persecution)</term> of the Lord, let him not think that it is anything great, or that he has done it all himself, or that he has worked that work through his own diligence and industriousness. Never think that you have outrun the Lord in virtue, according to the meaning <margin>Philippians 2:a</margin> of him who says: "It is He who works in you both the will and the accomplishment, according to His good pleasure."

**Question.** Why then does Scripture command man to do these things?

**Answer.** We have already said that man possesses by nature a certain industriousness of the spirit that seeks God. Therefore, He commands that man first know; and after he knows, then to love and bring the will forward. But the grace of the Lord grants to the willing and the believing that the spirit may be exercised, endure labor, or complete any work. Therefore, the human will is like an innate help of nature; when it is absent, God Himself does not work, even though He has the power; this occurs because of the freedom of the human will. Thus then

<summary>This page argues that while human effort and will are necessary components of spiritual life, they are secondary to the grace of the Holy Spirit, which provides the power for prayer and virtue. It emphasizes that God respects human free will and does not act within the soul unless the human will is first inclined toward Him.</summary>

### Of paradise and of the spiritual law.

...then the completion of the work belongs to God: but the will belongs to man. If we attribute the whole will to man: then the whole work is attributed to us. God is wonderful in all things / and he can in no way be understood with the intellect. But we humans labor to obtain some small part of his wonders / leaning on the scripture / or being taught by the same. <margin>Isaiah 4:d / 1 Corinthians 2:d</margin> For there it stands written: Who has known the mind of the Lord? Where the Lord has <margin>Luke 13:g</margin> said / "How often have I wanted to gather your children / and you have not willed it?" Believe at last from this / that he leads us by the hand: and desires from us only the will. For what explanation of the will shall there be / but willing labor? 

Just as iron, while it cleaves, cuts, plows, or plants, does as much as it is driven to do: but it is another who moves and stirs the iron: and when it is worn out / so he puts it into the fire / and refashions it <note>Original Dutch "rustet vveder-om op" suggests the blacksmith restoring the tool to a usable state through fire and forging.</note> again. So also the human, although he is exercised and although he labors / working that which is good: nonetheless the Lord works secretly in him: <margin>Acts 17:d</margin> and while he becomes weary and worn out with labor / so the Lord comforts him and refreshes <term>vermaeckt (refreshes or cheers the spirit)</term> his courage. <margin>Isaiah 10:d</margin> Shall the axe glory without the one who hews with it: or shall the saw raise itself without the one who pulls it? 

So it is also ordained in the pursuit of wickedness. When man is ready and prepared / and submits himself: then Satan comes and drives him forth / and sharpens him / just as the murderer sharpens the sword. We have compared the heart to iron / because of its great hardness / and its inexperience in these matters. Although we / like iron that has no feeling / ought not to be ignorant of him by whom we are governed: (for we fall quickly from the word of the farmer <note>The "farmer" here likely refers to the divine sower or the "husbandman" who cultivates the soul.</note> to the thought of wickedness) but just as

### HOMILY XXXVIII.

Many people are held to be Christians / because they seem righteous: but the craftsmen and the learned must judge / whether such people truly have the mark and the image of the king / or whether the works of the laborers are in any way falsified / or whether the craftsmen wonder at or rebuke them. But when that is taken away, then she <note>The original Dutch uses the feminine "si" (she), referring to the soul.</note> reveals and manifests herself to the Christians, and to those who love and seek Him in truth, just as Christ says, <margin>John 14:23</margin> "I will manifest myself to him, and will make my dwelling with him."

# Homily XXXVIII.

...those deemed worthy of some grace and, so to speak, sprinkled with a few droplets from the deep sea, find the same at every hour: and that daily such great and wonderful workings occur, that the one who experiences them—because of the unexpected, strange, and divine operation—is astonished and wonders by what reason he is bewildered. But divine and heavenly grace enlightens him everywhere, leads him, makes him peaceful and great: so that kings, the powerful, the wise, and the highly esteemed are considered very small and base things when they are compared with him. 

But shortly thereafter, at an unexpected time, things are changed, so that he thinks he is the greatest sinner above all men: at another hour he sees himself as a very great and powerful king, or a mighty friend of the king: at another moment, on the contrary, he sees himself weak and poor. Furthermore, the heart falls into doubt as to why it happens now this way and then that way again. For Satan, since he hates the good, breathes evil into those who follow virtue, and is intent on turning them away: for this is his work. 

But see to it that you do not become subject to him: and abide by the righteousness which is fulfilled in the inner man, where the altar of Christ stands within the undefiled holy place: so that the testimony of your conscience may glory in the cross of Christ, who has purified your conscience from dead works, so that you serve God with your spirit, so that you know what you worship, according to the meaning of <margin>John 4:22</margin> Him who said: "We worship that which we know." Trust in God who governs you: let your soul have communion with God, just as the bride has communion with the bridegroom. For this <margin>Ephesians 5:32</margin> <term>mystery</term><note>The Dutch "mysterie" here refers to a profound spiritual truth or a "sacred secret."</note> is great, he says. But I say, between Christ and the undefiled soul. To Whom be glory. AMEN.

# Why the divine scripture is given to us by God.

### HOMILY XXXIX.

Just as a King who writes letters to those to whom he desires to give <term>codicils</term><note>From the Latin *codicillus*, referring here to royal decrees or documents granting specific privileges and gifts.</note> and special gifts, commands them all thus: "Make sure that you come to me at once, so that you may receive royal gifts from me." If these people do not come and receive them, they shall be no better off for having read these letters; indeed, they shall be held as deserving of punishment, because they neglected to come and receive the gift of honor from the king's hands. In this way, God the King has sent the divine scriptures to mankind as letters: and has made known through them that, by calling upon God and believing, they should desire and receive the heavenly gift from the very substance of His divinity. 
<margin>2 Peter 1:4</margin><note>The original Dutch text cites "2. Pet, 1. a." following an older division system.</note> For there it is written: *That we might become partakers of the divine nature.* If, then, a person does not come, does not desire, and does not receive: then it shall not help him that he has read the scriptures; indeed, he shall be punished with death: because he did not wish to receive the gift of life from the heavenly king: without which no one can obtain the immortal life, which is Christ. To Him be the glory throughout eternity. AMEN.

### HOMILY XL.

ther, like a spiritual chain: prayer to love, love to joy, joy to gentleness, gentleness to humility, humility to service, service to hope, hope to faith, faith to obedience, obedience to simplicity. Just as, on the other hand, the vices hang one from another: hatred from anger, anger from pride, pride from vainglory, vainglory from unbelief, unbelief from hardness of heart, hardness of heart from neglect, neglect from slackness, slackness from laziness, laziness from impatience, impatience from lust, and so other members of the vices hang together. In the same way also regarding the good, the virtues remain and hang together. 

But the head of every good purpose, and the summit of honorable works, is to remain persistent in prayer, through which we may also obtain the other virtues daily, if we desire that from God. For from this comes to those who are held worthy the sharing of divine holiness and divine power, and the binding together of the dispositions of the mind through hidden love with the Lord. For he who constrains himself daily to be persistent in prayer is ignited by spiritual love into divine love and burning desire for God, and he receives the grace of perfection from the <term>sanctifying Spirit</term><note>The Dutch "heylichmaeckenden gheests" refers to the Holy Spirit in His role as the one who makes the believer holy (sanctification).</note>.

Just as with the soul—considering that it is one throughout all the members, yet shows its power above in the brain and moves below in the feet—so the Godhead contains all creatures, both the heavenly and those who are beneath the abyss. And she <note>The author uses feminine pronouns for the "Godhead" (godtheydt), following the grammatical gender of the Dutch word.</note> is abundant in the creatures, even though she is distinct from them, being unmeasurable and incomprehensible. So the Godhead then beholds mankind, and she governs all things with reason. For all that they do, they do through fear of God; but these are not all sons, kings, or heirs. In the world, there are some murderers, others fornicators, and others robbers; on the other hand, some give their goods to the poor. The Lord observes both these and those, and gives rest and reward to those who do well. For there are abundant measures, and small ones too.

<margin>Many degrees of hellish pains.</margin>

There is also a great difference in the light and in the glory. In the punishment, there are also sorcerers and poisoners, others who are murderers, and others who have not sinned so heavily. But those who say that there is only one kingdom and only one punishment, and that there are no degrees, they speak wrongly. For how many worldly people now remain stuck to spectacles <note>original: "gaep-spelen"; literally "gape-plays," referring to public theatrical performances or idle shows.</note> and other dishonest things? How many there are now also who pray to and fear God? God observes both these and those, and like a righteous judge, he prepares rest for the former, but punishment for the latter. Just as men who harness horses to wagons, who pull the reins and drive one against the other (for each does his best to fell his opponent to the earth and to overcome him); so also in the heart of those who struggle is the spectacle of evil spirits who wrestle against the soul, which struggle God and the angels behold.

### Homily XL.

Thus, at every hour, many new thoughts are created inwardly, both from the soul and from sin. For the soul is overflowing with hidden thoughts, which she brings forth and gives birth to in an instant. Sin also has many thoughts and exercises; indeed, at every moment, she puts forward new thoughts against the soul. For the rational spirit is the charioteer <term>waghe man: literally "wagon-man," this is a classic metaphor where the mind or spirit acts as a driver controlling the "horses" of the soul's desires and thoughts.</term> who prepares the chariot of the soul, holding the reins of the thoughts; and he runs there against Satan’s chariot, where he has also harnessed his chariot against the soul.

**Question.** If the soul is rest <note>The term "rest" (ruste) in this mystical context refers to a state of spiritual peace or the "Sabbath of the soul" where it finds its home in God.</note>, how is it then that some say we cannot pray, nor do they remain in prayer? **Answer.** If it happens that this rest comes abundantly, then she <note>The author refers to "rest" using feminine pronouns, following the Dutch "de ruste."</note> gives an affection of mercy and other services: namely, visiting the brothers and serving the Word. Nature also desires to go forth, to visit the brothers, and to speak the Word. For one finds nothing which, being thrown into the fire, retains its own nature; instead, it must itself be changed into fire. Just as if you throw small stones into the fire, then some living lime <note>original: "leuende calcks." In traditional masonry, "living" or "quick" lime is created by heating limestone in a kiln.</note> comes from it. But if you throw someone into the water, it often happens that he drowns and perishes in the midst of the sea, and is suffocated without ever appearing again. But he who goes in gradually, he desires to come up again, to take ship, and to come back to land again to visit the people who dwell on the earth. So it is also ordained spiritually. If someone goes into the deep of grace, he nevertheless thinks of his companions. Indeed, nature itself desires to come to the brothers, to fulfill love, and to confirm the Word.

## Of the innermost depth of the soul.

...that which is in the vessel is stirred and becomes boiling hot when the fire is lit from the outside: but if you put no wood under it, the fire begins to slacken and almost perishes. So also the grace which is the heavenly fire is in you: if you pray for it and direct your thoughts to love Christ, then you have in some sense stoked the wood under it, and your thoughts are changed into fire and are colored with the desire for God. But if the spirit withdraws itself, even though it is outside of you, yet it is also in you, and it appears to be outside of you. But if a slothful person gradually gives themselves over to worldly things and anxieties, then sin comes forth again, and embraces the soul, and begins to torment the whole person. The soul, then, thinking on its former rest <note>The state of spiritual peace and union with God discussed on the previous page.</note>, begins to become distressed and to mourn its misery longer. The spirit then reaches out again to the Lord: the first rest begins to approach him, and he begins to seek more fervently, and says: "I pray to you, Lord." Gradually, the kindling and delighting fire is given to the soul alongside this: just as a fishing rod <term>henghelroede: a fishing pole.</term> gradually draws the fish out from the deep. For if this did not happen, and if he did not taste the bitterness and death, how would he be able to distinguish the bitter from the sweet, and death from life, and thank the life-giving Father, Son, and Holy Spirit, for all eternity! Therefore, let no one think that the soul is entirely enlightened; there is still much fodder <term>voeders: literally "feed" or "fuel," referring here to the material that sustains sin.</term> of sin within, and man has need of much labor and weariness <note>Original: "vermoedtheydt." In this spiritual context, it refers to the exhausting effort and perseverance required in the struggle against sin.</note> alongside the grace that has been given to him. For this reason, divine grace begins to come into the soul in small parts, which otherwise could make the person perfect in a very short time once it has purified him; but so that it might test the person's intention, whether he holds entire love for God, having no fellowship with the evil one, but whether he gives himself entirely over to grace; so the soul receives help from a small accepted beginning, being tested according to time and circumstance, provided that it has caused grace no pain or contempt in any matter. Grace itself is also nourished in the soul and takes root to the deepest parts and thoughts; after the soul has been kept upright through many proposed causes and has cooperated with grace, until it is entirely swallowed up by heavenly grace, thereafter reigning in that same vessel <term>vaetken: a "little vessel," a common biblical and mystical metaphor for the human person or soul.</term>. Just as a great city, once it is laid waste, where the walls are broken down and which the enemy has taken, brings no benefit through its size: and therefore it is to be wished that it were strengthened with very good walls, so that the enemies might not enter into it: So also, without doubt, the souls who are adorned with knowledge and understanding and with a very sharp spirit, are like the great cities. Now we must further examine whether they are strengthened by the power of the Spirit: so that the enemies, coming into them, do not lay them waste. # Homily XLII.
S v

[...those] who had knowledge of matters were positioned like great and wide cities; but they were devastated by enemies, because they did not have the Spirit of God. All simple and common people <note>original: "idiote ende slechte menschen"; in the context of the time, "idiote" referred to unlearned or laypeople, and "slechte" meant plain or simple, rather than "bad."</note> who share in grace are like small cities, protected by the power of the cross. But they fall from grace and perish for two reasons: either because they do not patiently endure the afflictions <term>quellaedsien (afflictions, torments, or trials)</term> brought upon them, or because they allow themselves to be seduced by the pleasures of sin. For those who travel that path cannot pass through without temptations <note>original: "tentacien."</note>. Therefore, just as a poor woman and a queen endure the same pain and sorrow in childbirth; likewise, the land of both a rich man and a poor man cannot bring forth any worthy fruit unless it receives a suitable rain: so too in the workings of souls, neither the wise nor the rich prevail except through patience, afflictions, and constant labor. For the life of Christians must be like honey, which is sweet and admits nothing bitter or poisonous. Thus are these people kind-hearted <term>goedertieren (merciful, kind, or benevolent)</term>, whatever may befall them, whether prosperity or adversity, as the Lord says: "Be merciful, even as your heavenly Father [is merciful]." For that which hurts and defiles a man is within. For out of the heart come evil thoughts, as the Lord says.
<margin>Luke 7. e. Mat. 15 b</margin>
Since it is inward things that defile a man, the driving spirit of wickedness is inside the soul, creeping and advancing; this is the veil of darkness and the "old man," which those who flee to God must put off, and they must put on the heavenly and new man, which is Christ. Therefore, nothing external can harm a man except the spirit of darkness that lives, works, and dwells in the heart. So everyone must fight against their thoughts, so that Chris-

### Of the progress of the Christian man.

a person always remains clinging to the Lord, placing in Him his trust and hope: and even if the devils were as strong as the mighty mountains, nevertheless they shall be burnt away by prayer, like wax before the fire. Meanwhile, however, the soul has great strife and war against them. There are floods of dragons, mouths of lions, and fire inflaming the soul. Just as a person who is rooted in wickedness, and is drunk with the <term>spirit of errors (a state of spiritual confusion or being led astray)</term>, and is given over to murder or adultery, is not released from doing evil: So also do the Christian people who are baptized in the Holy Spirit become loose and free from sins. But those who, having obtained grace, are mixed with sin, they are still seized with fear, and pass through a horrible place. Just as merchants sailing at sea, even though they have obtained a good wind and a calm sea, but have not yet come to the harbor, nevertheless fear that a contrary wind might suddenly arise, and the sea be disturbed with waves, and the ship come into peril: So also Christian people, although the good wind of the Holy Spirit blows within them, nevertheless they worry that the wind of the opposing power might arise, and blow, and create a tempest and waves in their soul. Therefore we must very diligently take care that we come to the <note>original: "hauene der rusten"</note> harbor of rest, to the perfect world, to eternal life, and the eternal joy, to the city of the saints, to the heavenly Jerusalem, to the church of the firstborn. But unless someone has passed through these anxieties, he fears greatly that by chance that evil power might present some corruption in the middle of the journey. Just as a woman who has conceived has her little child inside in the darkness, so to speak, and in a confined place: if it is brought into the world at the proper time, it then sees

Take this also in the same way in spiritual matters. All those who have received the divine seed, they possess it in an invisible manner, and hide it in dark and perilous places, because of the indwelling sin: If they then guard the seed with all their might, they bring it properly into the light: furthermore, in the <term>dissolution of the body (death)</term> they are received with a joyful countenance by all the angels and heavenly assemblies. But if someone, having taken up the <term>weapons of Christ (the spiritual armor mentioned in the Bible)</term> to fight bravely, goes about it weakly, he is immediately handed over to the enemies at the time of the dissolution of his body, and from the darknesses that now overtake him, he goes to other, heavier darknesses, and to death.

### Of the Progress of the Christian Person

...floods of sins, which seek to call it away and claim it as their own. Therefore, if the heart is somewhat light-minded and wavers in impure thoughts, truly the spirits of error have taken their nourishment there; and entering in, they have overturned all that seemed clear, and brought to naught the good thoughts, and utterly devastated the soul. 

Just as the eye is the smallest part of all the parts of the body—and although the <term>apple of the eye (the pupil)</term> is small, yet it is a great vessel: for it beholds at once the heaven, stars, sun, moon, cities, and other creatures: and all that is seen is, all at once and at the same moment, formed and placed within that small apple of the eye: 

So it is also with the thoughts in the heart; and the heart is a small vessel, in which nevertheless are dragons, lions, venomous beasts, all treasures of wickedness, sharp and unclean paths, and high and steep places: but on the contrary, where God is, there also are the angels, the life and the kingdom, the light and the Apostles, the heavenly cities, the treasures of grace, and there are abundant things. 

For just as a dark fog spread over the entire earth makes it so that one person does not see another: so it is also with the darkness of this world, cast over all creatures and also over the whole of human nature from the time of the <term>transgression (the original sin of Adam and Eve)</term> onward: by which people are overshadowed in the night and in gruesome places. 

And just as in a house where there is much smoke: so sin sits and crawls with its foul thoughts into the thoughts of the heart, and with it an infinite multitude of demons. Just as also in matters that we see before our eyes: In times of war, the wise and the rulers do not go out to the battle, but they remain sitting at home, fearing death: but against the enemies are placed young warriors, poor and simple men: and it happens that they gain victory against the enemies, and serve

### Homily XLIV.

In this period, "subtle" described a substance that was refined, pure, and capable of penetrating other materials, often used to describe the nature of the Holy Spirit or the resurrected body.</note>, good, and heavenly Spirit, so that he might lift up and enlighten the soul, which is drowned in the deep waters of wickedness, and make it fly to the heights of heaven: and shall transport and change it from its nature: Just as it clearly appears that no one can, by their own power,

cross or go over the sea, unless he has a light and thin little ship made of wood, which alone can have passage upon the water: for if someone walks in the sea, they drown and perish: In the same way, the soul also cannot of itself sail through, cross, or pass over the bitter sea of sins, and the heavy abyss of the evil spirits of the darkness of the <term>affections</term><note>Original: "affectien." These are the unruly passions or worldly desires that weigh down the human heart, preventing spiritual progress.</note>: unless it receives the subtle, heavenly, and flying Spirit of Christ, walking and going over all wickedness: by whose benefit, and right and short course, it shall be able to come to the heavenly harbor of rest, to the city of the kingdom. Just as those who are in the ship do not scoop up nor drink from the sea, nor do they fetch food or clothing from there, but they carry them from elsewhere into the ship: So also the souls of Christians take not from this world, but from above out of heaven the heavenly food and the spiritual clothing: and living therefrom, they go into the ship of the good and life-giving Spirit, and they pass through the horrible opposing powers of the <term>princes and authorities</term><note>Original: "princen ende machtighen." And just as all ships are built from the nature of wood, with which men may sail over the troublesome sea: so also all Christian souls, strengthened with the single divinity of the heavenly light, with various gifts of grace, sail swiftly past all wickedness. # On the Change and Renewal in Mankind #

...to proceed happily: so the Lord himself is working in the believing soul, which he strongly, fiery, and calmly (as he well can) makes to sail through the heavy storms and <term>punishing waves</term><note>Original: "straf-baren." This refers to violent, crashing waves, used here as a metaphor for the harsh trials of a sinful life.</note> of wickedness, and through the power of the mighty winds of sins, undoing the tempests of the same. 

<margin>Wisdom 14</margin>
For without Christ the heavenly <term>steersman</term><note>Original: "stiman." A pilot or navigator. The text portrays Christ as the only one capable of guiding the "ship" of the soul.</note> no one can pass the evil sea by the power of darkness, and the blasts of the troublesome <term>temptations</term><note>Original: "tentacien." From the Latin "tentatio," meaning trials or lures that test one's faith.</note>. 

<margin>Matthew 14:c</margin>
For they climb up (says <margin>Psalm 106</margin> David) to the heavens, and they descend down to the depths. He who treads the punishing water-waves with his feet, knows all the art of steering, and of gusts and temptations. 

<margin>Hebrews 2:d</margin>
For, as it is written: he who has been tempted, is able to help those who are being tempted. Therefore our souls must be converted and changed from this present condition into another state and into the divine nature: we must from old humans become new, that is, from punishing and unbelieving, to good, meek, and believing: and so be made fit for the heavenly kingdom.

For Saint Paul writes thus of his conversion and <term>reception</term><note>Original: "opneminghe." This refers to Paul being "taken up" or accepted into Christ’s service.</note>, by which he was seized by the Lord: 

<margin>Philippians 3:c</margin>
"I pursue it, if I might somehow seize that for which I have been seized by Christ Jesus." 

How then is he seized by God? Just as if a tyrant led out some prisoners whom he had captured, and they were then regained by the true king. So also Paul, when he was tormented by the tyrannical spirit of sins, he persecuted and destroyed the <term>congregation of Christ</term><note>Original: "ghemeynte Christi." Referring to the early Christian Church.</note>; but because he did it not out of hatred for God, but through ignorance, as if fighting for the truth, he was not despised. Rather, the Lord has seized him, the heavenly and true King, where He was shining around him with a <term>hidden light</term><note>Original: "verholen licht." This refers to the miraculous light on the road to Damascus that blinded Paul but enlightened his soul.</note>.

### Homily XLIIII. ###

...and deemed him worthy of His voice, struck him in the face, and delivered the slave <note>This refers to the conversion of St. Paul on the road to Damascus; the "slave" is the soul previously enslaved to sin.</note>. Behold the loving-kindness and the conversion that the Lord employs, how He can change the souls that cling to sins and have become cruel, and transplant them in a short moment into His goodness and tranquility. For all things are possible with Him: just as happened to the murderer <note>original: "moordenaer." Traditionally referring to the Penitent Thief on the cross (Dismas).</note>, who in a short moment was converted through faith and transported into paradise. The Lord has come so that He might change and recreate our souls, and that He might make them partakers of the divine nature <note>A reference to 2 Peter 1:4, a central theme in Eastern Christian mysticism regarding "theosis" or divinization.</note>, as it is written; and that He might bring into our souls the heavenly soul—that is, the divine spirit—which leads us into all virtue, so that we may live the eternal life. 

Therefore we must believe His unspeakable promises with our whole heart: for He who has promised is truthful. And therefore we must love the Lord, and apply ourselves to all manner of virtues: and pray with <term>steadfastness</term><note>original: "stantvasticheyt." This implies a firm, unmoving endurance in spiritual practice.</note> and without ceasing, that we may wholly and perfectly obtain His promised spirit, so that our souls may be made alive: for we are still in the flesh. For unless the soul, with very great faith and prayer, receives in this world the <term>sanctification</term><note>original: "heylichmaeckinghe." The process of being made holy by the Holy Spirit.</note> of the spirit, and becomes a partaker of the divine nature, tempered with grace—by whose benefit she may purely and without <term>reproach</term><note>original: "berispinghe." Meaning without blame or being found at fault.</note> fulfill all the commandments—she is not fit for the kingdom of heaven. For whatever good that man may obtain from here now, that shall on that day be his life, through the Father, and the Son, and the Holy Spirit in eternity. AMEN.

### HOMILIE XLV.

<image-desc>Decorative square drop cap with the letter 'D' set against a floral background.</image-desc>
Those who have chosen a solitary life <note>original: "eensaem leue." This refers to the ascetic or monastic path of seeking God in quietude and withdrawal from worldly society.</note> must count all things that occur in this world as foreign and not concerning them. For he who follows the cross of Christ in truth must deny all things, yes, even his own soul; and he must fix his heart in the love of Christ, and hold the Lord above parents, brothers, wife, children, kinsmen, friends, and riches. For the Lord has clearly spoken this with these words: *Whoever does not leave father, mother, brothers, wife, children, fields, and does not follow me, he is not worthy of me.* For nowhere else is the <term>salvation</term><note>original: "salicheyt." This term implies both eternal soul-health and the state of being blessed.</note> and rest of men found, as we have heard. For how many kings have sprung from Adam's race who possessed the entire earth and gloried in royal power: yet none of them all, despite that abundant power over worldly things, was able to recognize the sin of the soul resulting from the transgression of the first man. By this transgression, the soul was so darkened that it no longer recognized its own transformation: namely, that when the soul was previously pure and placed in dignity, it saw its Lord; but now, because of the Fall, it has put on shame, having the eyes of the heart blinded so that they should not see the glory which our father Adam saw before he was disobedient.

While the modern word means "artists," here it refers broadly to any skilled master of a craft or science.</note> who spread and practiced their arts upon the world: some have turned wood, and with the lathe-tool carved kinds of birds and fish, and also images of men, laboring therein to demonstrate their dexterity: others have attempted to engrave likenesses and images and other things in copper: others have built great and magnificent houses: others have dug into the earth and found silver and gold, which is perishable: others have obtained precious stones: and others, adorned with beauty of body and exalting themselves through the fair appearance of the face, being more enticed by Satan, have fallen into sin. 

And all these aforementioned artisans have been possessed by the <term>serpent</term><note>original: "serpent." A symbolic reference to the devil or the corruptive nature of sin dwelling within the human heart since the Fall.</note> dwelling within them, yet they have not known that sin lived with them; and they have become prisoners and slaves of the evil power, for neither their knowledge nor their art has helped them. 

Therefore, the world—full of all sorts of diverse things—is like a rich man who has costly and great houses, gold and silver, many possessions, and an abundant family of every sort: when this man was seized in his whole body with sicknesses and pains, all his friends and kinsmen with all manner of riches were with him, but they were not able to deliver him from his sickness. Thus, no diligence of those who live in the world, nor brothers, nor wealth, nor power, nor all that has been recounted here, can [help] the soul that is smothered in sin and cannot see purely,

### How man is healed.

...be delivered from sin, except only through the presence of Christ <note>original: "Christi." Latin possessive for Christ.</note>, who can purify the soul and the body. Therefore let us be delivered from all the anxieties of this world, diligently seeking the Lord, calling to Him night and day: for this visible world and the rest found within it—which seem to nourish the body more sweetly—only cause the pains of the soul to become heavier and increase her sickness.

A certain wise man, desiring to pursue his purpose, did his utmost to search out all that went on in this world, so that he might in some way gain a profit: he came to kings, mighty lords, and princes: but from there he received no healing medicine that he could bring to the soul: he consorted with them for a long time but found no benefit: he went further to the wise and the eloquent of this world, and also departed from them, gaining no profit: after that he came to painters, and to those who bring gold and silver out of the earth: and finally to all <term>artisans</term><note>original: "constenaers." As noted previously, this refers broadly to masters of any craft, science, or technical skill.</note>: but he could find none who could heal his wounds: at the very last, when he had departed from these, he called upon God, who heals the <term>affections</term><note>original: "affectien." Referring to the disordered passions or emotional disturbances of the soul.</note> and the sicknesses of the soul. But when he began to reflect upon himself, and weighed these matters with reason, it happened that his heart began to hate those things with which he was previously occupied, and which he had entirely abandoned. 

Just as a woman rich in the world, though she has much money and magnificent houses, nonetheless has no authority: if men wish to rise up against her to harm her and destroy her <term>household</term><note>original: "huslinghen." This refers to the members of a household, including servants and family members.</note>, she cannot bear the oppression, but goes about searching for a powerful, capable, and suitable man: and after she has obtained such a man through much labor, she rejoices...

### Homily XLV.

...her for his sake and holds him as a strong wall. So also the soul, after the <term>transgression</term><note>original: "overtredinghe." ### HOMILY XLVI.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'H' featuring floral motifs begins the homily.</image-desc>
The word of God is God, and the word of the world is the world. But there is a great difference and distance between the word of God and the word of the world, and between the children of God and the children of the world: for every generation <note>meaning "kind" or "offspring"</note> is like its parents. Therefore, if a child of the spirit chooses to give itself over to the word of the world and the earthly concerns and glories of this world, it is killed and perishes, nor can it obtain the true rest of life: for its rest is situated in the place from where it was born. For the one who is held fast by worldly cares, and who is bound with worldly bonds, that person is smothered—just as the Lord says—and is made useless regarding the divine word. ### A spiritual explanation of certain things that occurred under the Law. <note>The "Law" refers to the Mosaic Law or the Old Testament era.</note>

## HOMILY XLVIII.

<margin>
I.
The glorious countenance of Moses.
</margin>
Such glory as Moses bore in his countenance was a prefiguration <note>A "pre-sign" or symbolic omen of something to come in the future.</note> of the true glory. For just as the Jews at that time could not behold the face of Moses with steady eyes: so Christians now receive the glory of the light in their soul: and the darknesses, which cannot endure the brightness of the light, are all blinded and driven away.

<margin>
II.
Circumcision.
</margin>
Furthermore, the Jews were known as God’s people through circumcision: but now God’s own people receive the sign of circumcision cut within their heart. For the spiritual sword cuts off the excess of the heart, that is, the impure foreskin <note>original: "onsuvuer sloopken" - literally "impure little casing," referring here to the spiritual equivalent of the prepuce.</note> of sins.

<margin>
III.
Baptism.
Matthew 3:11
</margin>
Among them, baptism made the flesh holy: but among us, it is the baptism of the Holy Spirit and of fire. For Saint John proclaimed this, saying: He shall baptize you in the Holy Spirit and in fire.

### Homily XLVII.

D ij

...is like. The lamb ought to be killed and sacrificed, and the doorposts smeared with blood. For Christ, the true, good, and unblemished lamb, was killed: and with His blood the thresholds and sills of the heart are smeared: so that the blood of Christ, shed upon the cross, comes to the life and redemption of the souls: but to the Egyptian devils, it brings sorrow and death. For in truth, the blood of the spotless lamb is sorrow to them: but to the soul, it is joy and rejoicing. Furthermore, after the anointing, he depicts the eating of the lamb in the evening, and the unleavened bread with wild lettuces <note>Original: "lattouwen", referring to the bitter herbs (Lactuca virosa) eaten during the Passover to symbolize the bitterness of slavery.</note>, while having the loins girded <note>Original: "nieren", literally "kidneys", which in biblical language often refers to the loins or the innermost seat of the will.</note>, and shoes on the feet, and staffs in the hands. For the soul is not permitted to eat of the lamb, unless she is first prepared in every way, as much as is possible for her, through good works. For although the lamb is sweet and the unleavened bread is good, nevertheless the lettuces are bitter and sharp. Therefore the soul eats of the lamb and of the lovely unleavened bread with very great pain and torment: because the sin that is present there torments her. He says that one shall eat it in the evening: but the evening hour is between light and dark. Thus also the soul, in her deliverance, walks between the light and the darkness, by the assistance of divine power, which does not allow the darkness to rise up against the soul and swallow it. And just as Moses said: "This is the night of God's promises": So also Christ, being in the synagogue when the book was given to him—as it is written—called it the acceptable year of the Lord, and the day of redemption. There was the night of restorations, and here the day of redemption: and with good reason. For all those things were figures and shadows of the truth: and, presented spiritually, they darkly signified true salvation.

# Spiritual interpretation of the law.

...of the soul, locked in darkness, secretly mended <note>Original: "ghebetert," which can mean improved, repaired, or reformed. In this context of a soul in a "pit," it suggests a restoration of the soul's nature before its final release.</note> in the deepest pit, and shut within metal gates: which could not be set free without **Christ** the Savior. So then He brings the soul out of **Egypt**, and out of the slavery where she is oppressed, killing in the departure the firstborn of **Egypt**. Now a portion of the power of the true **Pharaoh** is brought low. The **Egyptians** are sorrowful: for they sigh, suffering poorly at the salvation of the captives. He commands that one should borrow the gold and silver vessels from the **Egyptians**; and having received those, to go away thereafter. For while the soul departs from the darknesses, she receives silver and gold vessels; namely, her good thoughts, burning seven times more brightly <note>A reference to Psalm 12:6, where the words of the Lord are described as silver purified seven times; here, the soul’s thoughts are similarly refined.</note>, with which one serves God, and God rests therein. For the devils, who were her neighbors before, had possessed and stifled her thoughts. Blessed is the soul that is delivered from the darkness. And woe to the soul that does not cry out nor sigh to Him who can deliver her from the heavy and bitter taskmasters of [sinful] works. After the children of **Israel** have kept Passover, they go away: The soul progresses and prospers, she who has received the life of the Holy Spirit, who has tasted of the lamb, who is anointed with the lamb’s blood, and who has eaten the true bread, the living word. The pillar of fire and the pillar of cloud, guarding them, go before: the Holy Spirit confirms this one, feeding and gradually leading the soul. **Pharaoh** with the **Egyptians**, noticing the flight of the people of **Israel**, and that he is robbed of their service, has dared to pursue them even after the death of the firstborn. He very diligently harnessed his chariots, and traveled with all his people to destroy them: and when he was about to come among them, the cloud came between the two of them. <note>The Dutch "beyden" indicates the space between the pursuing Egyptians and the fleeing Israelites.</note>
