...both <note>The author refers to the pillar of cloud and fire</note>, which hindered and darkened the **Egyptians**, but brought light to the children of **Israel** and protected them. And so that I do not make it too long by recounting the entire History: take everywhere the comparison <note>original: "ghelijckenisse"</note> with spiritual things. For as soon as the soul flees out of **Egypt**, divine power comes and helps her, and leads her to the truth. 

But once the spiritual **Pharaoh**, the king of the darkness of sin, knows that the soul is turning away from him, and that her thoughts shun those things by which she was formerly held fast in his kingdom (for these are his powers): so this cruel enemy intended and hoped that she would return to him again. But when he perceived that the soul had taken flight entirely from his tyranny, he ran shamelessly toward her, despite the death of the firstborn and the theft of her thoughts <note>Referring to the previous page's allegory where the soul "borrows" or reclaims its thoughts from the enemy to serve God</note>, fearing that if the soul escaped completely, no one would be found to fulfill his will and his work. 

Therefore, he pursues her with tribulations <term>quellaedsien | historical Dutch term for torments, afflictions, or vexations</term>, temptations, and invisible warfare. Here the love is proved, tested, and marked, with which the soul embraces Him who has brought her out of **Egypt**: for she is given over to be proved and tested in every manner. For she sees the power of the enemy who wants to approach and kill: but he cannot do that: for between the soul and the **Egyptian** spirits stands the **Lord** in the middle. 

She sees before her the sea of bitterness, of opposition, and of despair: yet she cannot turn back, because she sees her enemies already prepared; nor can she go forward, because the fear of death, and the heavy and manifold tribulations with which she is oppressed, set death before her eyes. For this reason, the soul becomes despairing of herself, having the sentence of death within her <note>A reference to 2 Corinthians 1:9, describing a state of total reliance on God when all human hope is lost</note>, on account of the multitude of wickedness which

...all around her. Furthermore, after the soul is delivered from her enemies / and has passed the bitter sea / through the divine power: when she then sees from afar that the enemies / whom she recently served / are all destroyed and killed before her eyes: so she rejoices with an unspeakable and glorious joy / having received comfort from God / and rest in the Lord. The spirit which she has received then sings to God a new song with the drum of the body / and with the ingenious strings of the harp or of the soul / with very subtle thoughts / and with the sounding-bow <note>original: "clanck-booghken," likely referring to the bow of a stringed instrument or a musical bridge</note> of divine graces / and sends her songs of praise to the life-giving Christ <note>original: "Christum"</note>. For just as the blown breath going through the pipe makes a sound: even so the Holy Spirit praises / sings / and prays with pure hearts to God / through holy and spiritual people.

# Of the perfect faith in God.

...spiritual things: for the ancient practice is the shadow and the image of the present practice. <margin>All things of the Old Testament happened for the sake of the soul.</margin> Circumcision / tabernacle / ark / jar <term>eemer | an omer, a Hebrew measure used specifically for manna</term> / heavenly bread / indeed, everything that happened among the children of Israel, in the law of Moses / and in the times of the prophets / all happened for the sake of the soul / which was created in the image of God / but has fallen under the yoke of slavery / and under the kingdom of heavy darkness. For God desired to make communion with her / and has prepared her as His royal bride: He purifies her of filth / and washing away the blackness and ugliness / makes her shining / He makes mortality living / He heals brokenness / and pacifies her / reconciling all her enmity. For He stretches her out / and makes her grow to an infinite and immeasurable growth: until she becomes unspotted and a worthy bride for Him. At first He nurtures her by herself / and increases her through Himself / until she has obtained the full measure of His love. For since He is a perfect bridegroom / so He takes her as a perfect bride / into a holy spiritual and unspotted communion of the wedding: and then she reigns with Him in the endless ages, AMEN.

[unrighteous in] small things is also unrighteous in the greatest. What is this "small," and what is this "great"? The "small" refers to the promises belonging to this world: whereby the Lord has promised to give those who believe in Him food, clothing, and other things that contribute to a happy state and the health of the body. Concerning these, He has commanded that man should not be anxious, <note>The Dutch 'sorgvuldich' here carries the sense of being "full of care" or "worried," reflecting the biblical command in Matthew 6 not to worry about tomorrow.</note> but that he should believe, through the trust he has received from Him, that the Lord provides for all those who take their refuge in Him. The "great" refers to the gifts of the eternal and imperishable world, which He has promised to give to those who believe in Him, and who are therefore diligent without ceasing, and desire these same things from Him. For He has commanded, saying:

<margin>Matthew 6:33</margin>
> Seek first the kingdom of God and His righteousness, and all these things shall be added unto you:

so that everyone might be tested through these small and temporal things, [to see] whether he believes God, who has promised to give them, provided that we are not anxious for them, but instead are only diligent for the coming eternal things. Thus it is apparent that a man has faith in imperishable things, and truly seeks eternal goods, if he maintains pure faith regarding what was said before. For every man who is obedient to the word of truth must test and examine himself—or he must be judged and questioned by spiritual men—concerning how he has trusted and dedicated himself to God: whether it has surely happened in truth according to His word, or whether he only thinks he believes through the delusion and the conceit of the justifications and the faith which he holds within himself. For whether someone is faithful in the small is judged and shown by the perishable things. Listen to how this happens.

# On Perfect Faith in God

Do you say that you believe you will be made worthy of the kingdom of heaven, and that you are born from above as a child of God, and a co-heir of Christ <note>original: "mede-erfnaem Christi"</note>, and that you shall reign with Him for all ages, and enjoy delight in the heavenly light <note>The original "holen liecht" likely refers to the clear or radiant light of the divine presence.</note> for an infinite and uncountable eternity, just like God? 

To this you will say, "Certainly and without doubt: for it is for this reason that I have left the world and have given and promised myself to the Lord." 

Examine yourselves, then, whether you are not perhaps until now still occupied with earthly things; whether you are not anxious <note>The Dutch 'sorgvuldich' implies being "full of care" or "worried," suggesting a lack of trust in divine providence.</note> for the body’s food and clothing, and also for other occupations and matters, as if you could provide enough for yourself through your own power. Concerning these things, we are commanded to be in no way anxious. 

For if you believe that you shall receive the immortal and eternal things that remain forever and do not perish, how much more must you believe that the Lord will give you perishable and earthly things? For He has even given these to evil men, to the beasts, and to the birds. 

<margin>Matthew 6:25, 31-32</margin>
Just as He has commanded here to bear no care, saying: "Be not anxious what you shall eat or drink, or with what you shall be clothed; for all these things the Gentiles <note>original: "Heydenen"</note> seek." 

If you are anxious about these things, and do not trust yourself entirely to His word, then know that you do not yet believe that you shall receive the eternal goods, which is the kingdom of heaven. You only think that you have faith, but you are still found unfaithful in small and perishable things.

<margin>Matthew 6:25</margin>
And again: Just as the body is more than the clothing, so too is the soul more than the body. Do you believe then that your soul is healed through Christ from the constant and incurable—

See how you deceive yourself: for you think that you have faith, and yet you do not yet believe truly as you ought. 

For had you believed that the persistent and incurable wounds and the evil passions of the immortal soul are healed by Christ, you would also believe that He can heal the illnesses of the body and the temporal passions; and you would take your refuge in Him alone, and despise the cleverness and power of physicians. For He who created the soul has also made the body: and He who has healed the immortal soul can surely heal the body of temporal passions and illnesses. 

Upon this you will say to me: "To heal the body, God has given herbs of the earth and medicines: and He has prepared the offices <note>The "offices" (officien) here refers to the professional role or duty of doctors.</note> of physicians for the illnesses of the body, ordaining that the body, which is taken from the earth, should be healed through various things from the earth." ### HOMILY XLIX.

If anyone, for the love of God the Lord, leaves his own possessions and this world, abandoning the pleasures of the world, and the possession of temporal goods, and father and mother: and crucifies himself <note>original: "cruyſte"; referring to the ascetic practice of "taking up one's cross" through self-denial and mortification of the flesh.</note>, and becomes a stranger, poor and needy: But if he does not find in himself divine rest in place of the rest of this world: and the delights of the spirit in his own soul in place of perishable pleasures: and if he does not put on the garment of divine light in the inward man in place of perishable clothes: and if he does not surely know the heavenly company in his soul in place of this past and fleshly fellowship: and if he does not have the joy of the spirit within instead of the apparent joy of this world: and that he does not obtain in the soul the consolation of heavenly graces and divine satiety <note>A theological term for being spiritually "filled" or satisfied by the presence of God.</note>, so that the glory of God appears to him, as it is written: And in short, if he does not obtain in his own soul the pleasant, incorruptible delight in place of this perishable pleasure: truly this man has become salt that has lost its strength <note>A reference to Matthew 5:13. ### Homily 50.

<meta>The previous page concluded the fifty homilies of Macarius with a formal approval by the theological censor in Antwerp. This page begins a new section, likely an appendix or biographical note, citing the "Suda" (an ancient Greek encyclopedia) to provide historical context on the two famous monks named Macarius and the ascetic tradition.</meta>

# S V I D A S
<note>original: "ESVIDA"; this refers to the *Suda* (or *Suidas*), a massive 10th-century Byzantine Greek encyclopedia of the ancient Mediterranean world, which was a common source for biographical information in the Renaissance.</note>

THERE were two <term>Macariuses</term> <note>These are Macarius the Great (the Egyptian) and Macarius of Alexandria, both famous 4th-century "Desert Fathers" known for their extreme asceticism.</note>, resembling one another in name, in **spiritual practice**, in life, manner, and teaching. One was an **Egyptian**, very renowned for miracles and wondrous works, strict and stern, maintaining a spiritual reserve toward those who came to him. The other was an **Alexandrian**, resembling the Egyptian in every way; and in addition, he was amiable, well-spoken, and cheerful with those who visited him—so much so that through this he brought many young men to **spiritual practice**. **Evagrius** <note>Evagrius Ponticus, a highly influential monk and theologian who was a student of both Macariuses.</note> became a disciple of both of these men, following their way of life, etc.

---

->*Hebrews 11:37–38*<-
<note>original: "Hebr. xi. g." The "g" refers to the specific paragraph division used in Bibles before the modern verse system was standardized.</note>

THE holy men have, through **faith**, walked about in sheepskins and goatskins, suffering want, oppressed, and tormented—of whom the world was not worthy—wandering in desert places, in mountains and caves, and in the holes of the earth.

<summary>This page provides a biographical overview of the two monks named Macarius (the Egyptian and the Alexandrian) as recorded in the Suda lexicon, highlighting their different temperaments. It concludes with a biblical citation from Hebrews describing the hardships of ancient ascetic saints.</summary>

# Short summary of the Homilies described in this book.

### HOMILY I.

Spiritual interpretation of the visions described by the Prophet Ezekiel. page 1.

II. Of the kingdom of darkness, which is sin: and that man is delivered from it through the goodness of God alone. 12.

III. Of the peaceful and quiet conduct of the brothers among one another, and of inward struggle. 15.

IV. By what means one shall progress in the Christian life. 20.

V. How much the life of Christians differs from the life of other men. 39.

VI. How one shall pray. 49.

VII. Of the loving-kindness of Christ toward men: along with the answers to certain questions. 54.

VIII. Of that which happens to Christians while they pray. 57.

IX. That the divine promises and prophecies are fulfilled through manifold advancements and temptations: And that we are delivered from the assaults of the devils by clinging to God alone. 61.

X. The gifts of divine grace are preserved and increased through humility and diligence: but perish through pride and sloth. 68.

XI. That the power of the Holy Spirit places itself in the heart of man like a fire. 72.

XII. Of Adam’s state, after he had lost God’s image. 82.

XIII. What fruit God demands from a Christian man. 93.

XIV. Those who dedicate and surrender themselves to God do not do so without hope of reward. 94.

XV. How holily and purely the Christian soul must present itself to Christ her bridegroom. 98.
<note>The metaphor of the soul as a bride to Christ was a central theme in medieval and early modern mystical theology.</note>

XVI. That spiritual men are subject to temptations and afflictions, of which the first sin <note>Original: "d'ierſte ſonde," referring to the Fall of Man or Original Sin.</note> is the origin. 130.

<summary>This page provides a table of contents for the first sixteen homilies of the collection, outlining key themes such as Ezekiel's vision, the nature of sin, the necessity of humility, and the spiritual marriage between the soul and Christ.</summary>
<keywords>Table of Contents, Ezekiel, Kingdom of Darkness, Christian life, prayer, divine grace, humility, Holy Spirit, Adam, spiritual marriage, temptations</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Table of Contents for the fifty homilies attributed to Macarius the Egyptian. The previous page covered Homilies I through XVI. This page outlines the themes of spiritual warfare, the dignity of the soul, and the inner glory of the believer, leading up to the start of Homily XXXIII.</meta>

XVII. Of the spiritual anointing and glory of Christians 139.

XVIII. Of the treasure of Christians, which is Christ, and the Holy Spirit exercising the same in various manners to attain perfection 148.

XIX. A Christian man who wishes to profit <note>In this context, "profit" refers to spiritual growth or advancement in the faith.</note> must drive himself to all that which is good 155.

XX. Christ alone has healed the soul, which he has clothed with the ornament of his grace 161.

XXI. A double war approaches the Christian man, inward and outward. 166.

XXII. Of the twofold state of those who die 169.

XXIII. Those who are of the royal seed, they alone may wear the royal crown 170.

XXIIII. The state of Christians is like unto commerce and practice. 172.
<note>The author uses the metaphor of "commerce" (*coopmanfchap*) to describe the active exchange of earthly effort for heavenly grace.</note>

XXV. Through disobedience we are brought into slavery, from which we are delivered through the mystery of the cross: Furthermore, of the power of divine tears, and of the divine fire 176.

XXVI. Of the dignity, power, and workings of the soul: and how it is tempted by the devil, and is delivered therefrom 182.

XXVII. Of the dignity and the state of the Christian man, and of his free will: with useful questions posed in between 198.
<note>The role of "free will" (*vrijen vville*) was a major point of discussion in these homilies, emphasizing that while grace is necessary, man must also choose to cooperate with God.</note>

XXVIII. Description and lamentation of the wretched souls in which Christ does not dwell because of sin. 213.

XXIX. God grants his grace in two ways, and he demands the fruits thereof back again with righteous judgment 217.

XXX. The soul that shall enter into the kingdom of God must be reborn of the Holy Spirit, and how this happens. 222.

XXXI. The believing man must be changed in spirit, and must gather all his thoughts in God, upon which divine service truly depends. 228.

XXXII. The glory of Christians is now hidden in their souls, which shall come forth at the time of the resurrection, and shall enlighten the bodies, each according to its measure 232.

XXXIII. How attentively and diligently one ought

This final section focuses on the transformation of the believer through Christ and the ultimate distinction between spiritual and worldly life.</meta>

ought to pray 239.

XXXIIII. Of the glory of Christians, which is not unknown to them. 241.

XXXV. Of the old and the new Sabbath-day 244.
<note>In these homilies, the "new Sabbath" typically refers to the spiritual rest found in Christ rather than a specific day of the week.</note>

XXXVI. Of the varied glory of those who rise again. 245.
<note>This refers to the "resurrection" (*verrijfenis* in the old spelling) and the different degrees of heavenly reward.</note>

XXXVII. Of paradise and of the spiritual law 246.

XXXVIII. One must have perfect wisdom in discerning true Christians, and who they are 255.

XXXIX. For what purpose the divine Scripture is given to us by God 258.

XL. The binding together of the virtues among one another, and likewise also of the vices 258.
<note>The author argues that virtues are linked like a chain; if one is truly present, others follow. The same is said of sins.</note>

XLI. How deep the innermost grounds of the soul are, which immediately take on the growth of grace or of sins. 262.

XLII. Internal things, not external things, advance or damage man: such as the spirit of grace or the spirit of wickedness 264.

XLIII. Of the progress of the Christian man, whose entire power depends upon the heart, as is variously described here. 266.

XLIIII. Of the great change and renewal that Christ, who has healed the sicknesses of the soul, works in the Christian man. 272.

XLV. That man is healed not by any arts or riches of this world, but solely through the coming of Christ: furthermore, the great communion of man with God is recounted here 278.

XLVI. Of the difference between the word of God and the word of the world: and between the children of God and the children of the world 283.

XLVII. Spiritual interpretation of certain things that occurred under the Law. 287.
<note>The "Law" here refers to the Old Testament; the author provides allegorical meanings for ancient Jewish customs.</note>

XLVIII. Of perfect faith in God 296.

XLIX. It is not enough to forsake the pleasures of this world, unless one obtains the salvation of the other world 301.

L. God is the one who performs miracles through his saints and creatures 304.

<page-num>148</page-num>
<header>Of the treasure of Christians.</header>

<margin>
How the purity of heart is to be obtained.
</margin>
...those who do not receive it. For this reason, one must diligently search and examine how and by what means the purity of heart is to be obtained. Truly, it is through no other than Him who was crucified for us. For He is the way, the life, the truth, the door, the pearl, and the living and heavenly bread; nor can anyone, without that truth, recognize the truth and be saved. Just as you then have renounced the outward man <note>The "outward man" refers to the physical body and worldly desires, as opposed to the "inward man" or the soul.</note> and all that is beheld with the eyes, and have distributed your goods and riches: so also concerning the wisdom of the world; if it be that you have learning and understanding of many things, or that you are eloquent in speech: all this you must cast away and account as nothing, so that you may thus be built upon the foolishness of preaching <note>original: "dwaelheydt der predicatien"; a reference to 1 Corinthians 1:21, where the Gospel is described as "foolishness" to the worldly wise but power to the believer.</note>: which preaching is the upright truth, without any grandeur of words, but having the power that works through the holy cross. Glory be to the Trinity of one substance in eternity. 
->A M E N.<-

->Of the treasure of Christians, which is Christ, and the Holy Spirit exercising the same in various ways to obtain perfection.<-

# HOMILY XVIII.

If someone in the world, being very rich, possesses a secret treasure, he obtains with this treasure and with the riches he has all that pleases him: and all possessions that he desires in the world, he easily attains those by means of the enjoyment of this treasure: for by the benefit of it, he acquires without any trouble all possessions that are pleasant to him. So also those who desire from God, they find and obtain the heavenly treasure of the spirit,

<vocab>Christians, purity, crucified, truth, Homily, treasure, spirit</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes a discourse on achieving purity of heart through Christ rather than worldly wisdom, before beginning Homily XVIII, which compares the Holy Spirit to a hidden treasure that provides for all spiritual needs.</summary>
<keywords>purity of heart, Christ as the Way, foolishness of preaching, Holy Trinity, spiritual treasure, Homily XVIII, Holy Spirit</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the text of Homily XVIII, expanding on the metaphor of Christ as a "heavenly treasure" introduced at the end of the previous page. The discussion focuses on how this internal spiritual wealth enables the believer to fulfill God's commandments with ease.</meta>

### HOMILY XVIII.

of the Spirit; namely the Lord himself who shines clearly and brightly in their hearts: and they bring forth from this treasure that is within them, which is Christ himself, all the righteousness of the virtues, and all the possessions of the goodness of the commandments of the Lord: and gather through him greater heavenly riches. For by the benefit of the heavenly treasure they fulfill through their works all the virtue of righteousness, enjoying the abundance of the spiritual riches that are with them: and very easily fulfill all justice and God's commandments, through the invisible riches of the grace <term>grace (gratien): the unmerited favor and divine assistance given by God</term> that is within them.

<margin>2 Corinthians 4</margin>
As Paul also says: Having this treasure in earthen vessels <note>A reference to 2 Corinthians 4:7; the "earthen vessels" represent human frailty and the physical body.</note>: that is, those who have been granted to obtain it while they still remain in the flesh, namely the sanctifying power of God.

<margin>1 Corinthians 1</margin>
And again: Who has become for us wisdom from God, righteousness, sanctification, and redemption. He who then finds and possesses within himself this heavenly treasure of the Spirit, fulfills purely and without reproach, easily and without any strain, through the benefit of that treasure, all the righteousness of the commandments and all the workings of the virtues. Let us therefore also heartily pray to God and desire that it may please him to give us the treasure of his Spirit: so that we may thus live purely and without reproach in all his commandments, and purely and perfectly fulfill all the righteousness of the Spirit, through the benefit of the heavenly treasure, which is Christ. For a poor beggar suffering hunger can obtain nothing in the world when poverty oppresses him. But he who enjoys the treasure, as was said before, obtains all the possessions he desires very easily and without labor. So also the naked soul, being forsaken by the company of the Spirit and brought under the heavy beggary <note>In this context, "beggary" refers to a state of spiritual bankruptcy or helplessness caused by sin.</note> of sins, cannot (even if it wishes to) [attain...]

<summary>This page describes Christ as an internal "heavenly treasure" that empowers believers to easily live virtuously and fulfill God's laws. It contrasts this spiritual wealth with the "beggary" of a soul lacking the Holy Spirit's presence.</summary>
